The context of diffusions as scaling limits, tolerant testing in the diffusion model and the stability of the test

The aim of this section is to illustrate on the basis of the stochastic SIS model the interplay of similarity testing in the diffusion approximation and the stability property.

C.1
The stochastic SIS model and its diffusion scaling limit

We consider a population of size
N
consisting of susceptible (S) and infected (I) people. The SIS model is characterized by two possible events, namely that a susceptible individuum gets infected when meeting an infected individuum and an infected one recovers and is again susceptible. This is described via a contact rate
\alpha
and a recovery rate
\beta
, see Chapter
3
in
[
38
]
. Denote
I_{\alpha,\beta}^{N}=(I_{\alpha,\beta}^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
the number of infected people in a population of size
N
. Then this number is given in the SIS model by the time-change equation

I_{\alpha,\beta}^{N}(t)=I_{\alpha,\beta}^{N}(0)+Y_{1}\left(\alpha\int_{0}^{t}I_{\alpha,\beta}^{N}(s)(1-I_{\alpha,\beta}^{N}(s)/N)ds\right)-Y_{2}\left(\beta\int_{0}^{t}I_{\alpha,\beta}^{N}(s)ds\right)

where
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
are two independent unit-rate Poisson processes (see Example
2.1
and p.21 in
[
4
]
). Let
x_{\alpha,\beta}^{N}:=I_{\alpha,\beta}^{N}/N
be the proportion of infected people. Assuming that
I_{\alpha,\beta}^{N}(0)/N\rightarrow x_{\alpha,\beta}(0)
as
N\to\infty
for some
x_{\alpha,\beta}(0)\in\mathbb{R}
, Theorem
4.1
in
[
4
]
shows that
(x_{\alpha,\beta}^{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
converges uniformly on compact sets in probability to the solution
x_{\alpha,\beta}
of

x_{\alpha,\beta}(t)=x_{\alpha,\beta}(0)+\int_{0}^{t}\alpha x_{\alpha,\beta}(s)(1-x_{\alpha,\beta}(s))-\beta x_{\alpha,\beta}(s)ds.

This evolution equation has
x_{\alpha,\beta}^{*}=0
and
x_{\alpha,\beta}^{*}=\frac{\alpha-\beta}{\alpha}
as equilibria. Moreover, the process

U_{\alpha,\beta}^{N}:=\sqrt{N}(x_{\alpha,\beta}^{N}-x_{\alpha,\beta})

converges weakly to
U_{\alpha,\beta}=(U_{\alpha,\beta}(t))_{t\geq 0}
solving

\displaystyle U_{\alpha,\beta}(t)

\displaystyle=U_{\alpha,\beta}(0)+W_{1}\left(\alpha\int_{0}^{t}x_{\alpha,\beta}(s)(1-x_{\alpha,\beta}(s))ds\right)+W_{2}\left(\beta\int_{0}^{t}x_{\alpha,\beta}(s)ds\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+\int_{0}^{t}\alpha(1-2x_{\alpha,\beta}(s))U_{\alpha,\beta}(s)-\beta U_{\alpha,\beta}(s)ds

with two independent Brownian motions
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
(see
(4.8)
and its derivation in
[
4
]
).
If
x_{\alpha,\beta}(0)=x_{\alpha,\beta}^{*}=\frac{\alpha-\beta}{\alpha}
, then
U_{\alpha,\beta}
solves

dU_{\alpha,\beta}(t)=-(\alpha-\beta)U_{\alpha,\beta}(t)dt+\sqrt{\frac{2\beta(\alpha-\beta)}{\alpha}}dW(t)

for some Brownian motion
W
, see also Section
3.5
in
[
38
]
for the derivation of this Ornstein–Uhlenbeck process.

SIS model
U_{\alpha,\beta}^{N}

Diffusion approximation
U_{\alpha,\beta}

Nonparametric diffusion approximation
U

Data generating process
U^{N}

scaling limit
tolerance
\eta
imposed scaling limit

Fig 3:
Prototypical example motivating the interplay of tolerant testing in a diffusion approximation and the stability of a tolerant test.

C.2
Tolerant testing in the diffusion approximation and stability

Although widely used in epidemiology, the SIS model is by far too simplified in order to capture the full dynamics and can adequately describe the data at most within a certain tolerance. Suppose we want to infer on deviations of the SIS model
U_{\alpha,\beta}^{N}
from the real phenomenon
U^{N}
within tolerance
\eta
. Imposing that
U^{N}
likewise has a diffusion approximation
U
motivates applying a similarity test developed for diffusion models, i.e. to test the tolerant null hypothesis against the alternative that
U_{\alpha,\beta}
deviates from
U
at tolerance
\eta
, see Figure
3
. This approach is indeed justified by a stability property with respect to the diffusion approximation which allows to transfer (at least for large
N
and within some small error) the results of the test for the diffusion model to the original jump process model. Note that in this example, such stability has to cover approximation schemes with laws singular to the one of the diffusion limit as the law
\mathcal{L}(U_{\alpha,\beta}^{N})
of
U_{\alpha,\beta}^{N}
is singular to the law
\mathcal{L}(U_{\alpha,\beta})
of
U_{\alpha,\beta}
- even after linear interpolation to make the sample paths continuous.

D
Preliminaries on scalar ergodic diffusion processes

Following Section
2
, we consider the stochastic differential equation (
2.1
) of the form

dX_{t}=b(X_{t})dt+\sigma dW_{t},\hskip 10.00002ptX_{0}=\xi

for a standard Brownian motion
W
, initial condition
\xi\sim\mu_{b}
independent of
W
and a drift function
b
belonging to the class
\Sigma(C,A,\gamma,\sigma)
. We recall its definition for fixed constants
A,\gamma,\sigma>0
and
C\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\Sigma(C,A,\gamma,\sigma)

\displaystyle:=\left\{b\in\textrm{Lip}_{\textrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}):\ |b(x)|\leq C(1+|x|)\ \forall x\in\mathbb{R}{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\frac{1}{2}}\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\left.\textrm{ and }\frac{b(x)}{\sigma^{2}}\textrm{sign}(x)\leq-\gamma\ \forall|x|\geq A\right\}.

Here,
\textrm{Lip}_{\textrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R})
denotes the class of all functions
f:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a constant
L_{n}>0
such that

|f(x)-f(y)|\leq L_{n}|x-y|\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{ for all }x,y\textrm{ with }|x|,|y|\leq n.

As noted in Section
2
, for each
b\in\Sigma(C,A,\gamma,\sigma)
the diffusion
X
admits the invariant density

q_{b}(x):=\frac{1}{C_{b,\sigma}}\exp\left(\int_{0}^{x}\frac{2b(u)}{\sigma^{2}}du\right)\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{ for all }x\in\mathbb{R},

with the normalizing constant

C_{b,\sigma}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\exp\left(\int_{0}^{x}\frac{2b(u)}{\sigma^{2}}du\right)dx,

where for
x<0
the integrals should be read as
\int_{0}^{x}f(u)du=-\int_{x}^{0}f(u)du
.
Some regularity properties of
q_{b}
are already determined by
b
. In particular, it is easily seen that
q_{b}
is differentiable and for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

q_{b}^{\prime}(x)=2\sigma^{-2}b(x)q_{b}(x).

Moreover, an important property that is made use of in several steps, is that
q_{b}
and
q_{b}^{\prime}
can be uniformly upper bounded over
\Sigma(C,A,\gamma,\sigma)
, whereas on the other hand on the inverval
[-A,A]
, the invariant density
q_{b}
is uniformly bounded away from zero.

Lemma D.1
(
[
9
]
, Lemma 3.2.1 and 3.2.2).

There exist two constants
L^{*}=L^{*}(C,A,\gamma,\sigma)<\infty
and
L_{*}=L_{*}(C,A,\gamma,\sigma)>0
such that

\sup_{b\in\Sigma(C,A,\gamma,\sigma)}\max\left\{\|q_{b}\|_{\infty},\|q_{b}^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\right\}\leq L^{*}

and

\inf_{b\in\Sigma(C,A,\gamma,\sigma)}\inf_{x\in[-A,A]}q_{b}(x)\geq L_{*}.

Another very important property is that the normalized local time
\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}T}L_{T}^{\cdot}(X)
approximates the invariant density
q_{b}
in the following sense.

Proposition D.2
(
[
1
]
, Corollary
14
).

Let
b\in\Sigma(C,A,\gamma,\sigma)
. Then there exist constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that, for any
p,T\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\sup_{b\in\Sigma(C,A,\gamma,\sigma)}\left(\mathbb{E}_{b}\left[\left\|\frac{1}{T\sigma^{2}}L_{T}^{\cdot}(X)-q_{b}\right\|_{\infty}^{p}\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt\leq c_{1}\left(\frac{p}{T}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\left(1+\sqrt{p}+\sqrt{\log T}\right)+Te^{-c_{2}T}\right).

Remark D.3
.

The preceding Proposition
D.2
was proven for our setup where the diffusion
X
is started in the invariant density. In Section
6
we need to consider the case where it is started in some fixed
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
to compare it with a fractional diffusion. For this, it is crucial that our results of Section
5
are still true under this assumption, which is also interesting on its own, see Remark
5.5
. This can be established by showing that Proposition
D.2
also works for
X_{0}=x_{0}\in[-A,A]
. A proof of this is given in detail in Section
3.2.3
of
[
9
]
.

By Markov’s inequality we get as a direct corollary of Proposition
D.2
that for every
\epsilon>0
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{b\in\Sigma(C,A,\gamma,\sigma)}\mathbb{P}_{b}\left(\left\|\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}T}L_{T}^{\cdot}(X)-q_{b}\right\|_{\infty}>\epsilon\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle T\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}0.

(D.1)

With this result, we can deduce a uniform version of the weak law of large numbers for a bounded class of functions having compact support.

Proposition D.4
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a class of functions that are bounded uniformly by some constant
C_{\mathcal{F}}
and supported in
[-A,A]
. Then for every
\epsilon>0
,

\lim_{T\to\infty}\sup_{b\in\Sigma(C,A,\gamma,\sigma)}\mathbb{P}_{b}\left(\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\left|\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}f(X_{s})ds-\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(z)q_{b}(z)dz\right|>\epsilon\right)=0.

By the occupation times formula,

\displaystyle\sup_{b\in\Sigma(C,A,\gamma,\sigma)}\mathbb{P}_{b}\left(\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\left|\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}f(X_{s})ds-\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(z)q_{b}(z)dz\right|>\epsilon\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\sup_{b\in\Sigma(C,A,\gamma,\sigma)}\mathbb{P}_{b}\left(\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(z)\left(\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}T}L_{T}^{z}(X)-q_{b}(z)\right)dz\right|>\epsilon\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq\sup_{b\in\Sigma(C,A,\gamma,\sigma)}\mathbb{P}_{b}\left(\left\|\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}T}L_{T}^{\cdot}(X)-q_{b}\right\|_{[-A,A]}\ \sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|f(z)|dz>\epsilon\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq\sup_{b\in\Sigma(C,A,\gamma,\sigma)}\mathbb{P}_{b}\left(\left\|\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}T}L_{T}^{\cdot}(X)-q_{b}\right\|_{[-A,A]}>\frac{\epsilon}{2AC_{\mathcal{F}}}\right),

where we used that by assumption
\int_{\mathbb{R}}|f(z)|dz\leq 2AC_{\mathcal{F}}
uniformly in
f\in\mathcal{F}
. Now, the last probability converges to zero for
T\to\infty
by (
D.1
) and the claim follows.
∎

E
Proof of Theorem
3.1
and general multiscale theory

This part of the appendix is organized in the following way: In Subsection
E.1
we present the multiscale theory that is used in our context, where the key result is Theorem
E.4
. In Subsection
E.2
we then provide the proof of Theorem
3.1
as an application of this Theorem
E.4
. In addition, we use it to prove finiteness of the random variable
S_{b}
given in Theorem
7.1
.

E.1
Auxiliary results from empirical process theory

The key ingredient for the proof of Theorem
3.1
will by a general result about the supremum of a stochastic process
Z=(Z(s))_{s\in\mathcal{S}}
that is defined on a totally bounded metric space
(\mathcal{S},\rho)
. Totally bounded means that for arbitrary
u>0
the capacity number

D(u,\mathcal{S},\rho):=\max\left\{\#\mathcal{S}_{0}:\mathcal{S}_{0}\subset\mathcal{S},\rho(s,t)>u\textrm{ for different }s,t\in\mathcal{S}_{0}\right\}

is finite, where
\#S
denotes the cardinality of a finite set
S
. Additionally, we consider a function
\sigma:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow(0,1]
, where
\sigma(s)
measures the spread of
X(s)
. We assume that

\displaystyle|\sigma(t)-\sigma(s)|\leq\rho(s,t)\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{ for all }\ s,t\in\mathcal{S}

(E.1)

and

\displaystyle\left\{t\in\mathcal{S}:\ \sigma(t)\geq\delta\right\}

(E.2)

is compact for any
\delta\in(0,1]
. A result of this kind was first established by Dümbgen and Spokoiny in
[
21
]
to derive a multiscale test in a regression setting and substantially refined by Dümbgen and Walther in
[
20
]
. A setup with random metrics and time-dependent metric spaces
\mathcal{S}_{T}
was treated by Rohde in
[
44
]
. In our scenario, the semimetric
\rho
and the spread measure
\sigma
are random and depend on the time horizon
T
, but are defined on the same fixed semimetric space
\mathcal{S}
for all
T
. We follow the proofs given in technical report
[
20
]
to weaken the assumption on the tails of
Z
, by allowing for an additional
\log
-factor compared to Theorem
8
and Corollary
9
in this reference
[
20
]
.

Proposition E.1
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be countable,
L>0
be some constant, and for
\delta>0
let
G(\cdot,\delta)
be a nondecreasing function on
[0,\infty)
such that for all
\lambda\geq 0
and
s,t\in\mathcal{S}
with
\rho(s,t)\geq\delta
we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{|Z(s)-Z(t)|}{\rho(s,t)}>G(\lambda,\delta)\right)\leq Le^{-\lambda}.

Then for arbitrary
\delta>0
and
a\geq 1

\mathbb{P}\left(|Z(s)-Z(t)|\geq 12J(\rho(s,t),a)\textrm{ for some }s,t\in\mathcal{S}\textrm{ with }\rho(s,t)\leq\delta\right)\leq\frac{L\delta}{2a},

where

J(\epsilon,a):=\int_{0}^{\epsilon}G\left(\log\left(\frac{aD(u,\mathcal{S},\rho)^{2}}{u}\right),u\right)du.

For finite
\mathcal{S}
the statement follows by Theorem
7
in the technical report
[
20
]
since the topology induced by the metric then coincides with the discrete topology with respect to which
Z
is always continuous. For countable
\mathcal{S}
, the proposition is then a consequence of the theorem of monotone convergence.
∎

The following theorem is an extension of Theorem
8
in the technical report
[
20
]
that establishes this result for random metrics and spread measures and weakens the tail assumption by an additional log-term.

Theorem E.2
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be countable and suppose that the following three conditions are satisfied:

(a)

There exists a constant
c_{0}>0
such that for (possibly) random function
\tilde{\sigma}:\Omega\times\mathcal{S}\rightarrow(0,1]
and random metric
\tilde{\rho}:\Omega\times\mathcal{S}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
which satisfy
(
E.1
)
,

\frac{1}{c_{0}}\leq\frac{\tilde{\sigma}}{\sigma}\leq c_{0}\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{1}{c_{0}}\leq\frac{\tilde{\rho}}{\rho}\leq c_{0}.

(b)

There exists a constant
L>0
such that for arbitrary
\lambda\geq 1
we have

\mathbb{P}\left(|Z(s)|\geq\tilde{\sigma}(s)\lambda\right)\leq L\exp\left(-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}+\log(\lambda)\right).

(c)

There exist positive constants
A_{0},B,V>0
such that

D\left(u\delta,\{t\in\mathcal{S}:\tilde{\sigma}(s)\leq\delta\},\tilde{\rho}\right)\leq A_{0}u^{-B}\delta^{-V}\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{ for all }u,\delta\in(0,1].

For a constant
Q>0
define the events

\mathcal{A}(Q,\delta):=\left\{\sup_{s,t\in\mathcal{S}:\tilde{\rho}(s,t)\leq\delta}\frac{|Z(s)-Z(t)|}{\tilde{\rho}(s,t)\log(e/\tilde{\rho}(s,t))}\leq Q\right\}.

Then there exist constants
C_{0}=C_{0}(A_{0},B,V,Q,c_{0})>0
and
0<\delta_{0}\leq 1
such that for
0<\delta\leq\delta_{0}
the probability of the event

\displaystyle\left\{|Z|\leq\tilde{\sigma}\sqrt{2V\log(1/\tilde{\sigma})+C_{0}\log\log(e/\tilde{\sigma})}+C_{0}\tilde{\sigma}\log(e/(C_{0}\tilde{\sigma}))^{-1}\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 199.16928pt\left.{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\sqrt{V}}\textrm{ on }\{s\in\mathcal{S}:\tilde{\sigma}(s)\leq\delta\}\right\}

is at least
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}(Q,2\delta))-C_{0}\log(e/(2c_{0}^{2}\delta))^{-\frac{1}{2}}
.

Remark E.3
.

The proof will show that an additional term
+c\log(\lambda)
in condition (b) of Theorem
E.2
can only be added to the exponent
-\lambda^{2}/2
for
c<2
.

We define a set
\mathcal{S}^{*}
inductively as follows: Let
s_{1}
be any point in
\mathcal{S}
maximizing
\sigma
. Next, let
u
be some continuous, non-decreasing function from
(0,1]
into iteself that will be specified later. Suppose we already picked
s_{1},\dots,s_{m}
and the set

\mathcal{S}^{\prime}:=\left\{s\in\mathcal{S}:\ \min_{i=1,\dots,m}\rho(s,s_{i})\geq u(\sigma(s))\sigma(s)\right\}

is non-empty. Then we define
s_{m+1}
to be an element of it with maximal value
\sigma(s)
, which is well-defined as the displayed set
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
is closed and
\{\sigma\geq\delta\}
is compact for any
\delta>0
by (
E.2
). Following this procedure we end up with a finite or countable set
\mathcal{S}^{*}=\{s_{1},s_{2},\dots\}
and its construction entails that

\sigma(s_{1})\geq\sigma(s_{2})\geq\dots

An important property of this set
\mathcal{S}^{*}
is that for any
s\in\mathcal{S}
there exists a point
t\in\mathcal{S}^{*}
such that

\displaystyle\sigma(s)\leq\sigma(t)\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt\rho(s,t)<u(\sigma(s))\sigma(s).

(E.3)

This can be seen in the following way: for
s\in\mathcal{S}
let
m
be a maximal index such that
\sigma(s_{m})\geq\sigma(s)
. If
\rho(s,s_{i})\geq u(\sigma(s))\sigma(s)
for every
i\leq m
, then
s
would belong to the set
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
, whence
\sigma(s_{m+1})\geq\sigma(s)
contradicting the definition of
m
.
For
0<\delta\leq 1
define

\mathcal{S}^{*}(\delta):=\left\{s\in\mathcal{S}^{*}:\ \frac{c_{0}\delta}{2}<\sigma(s)\leq c_{0}\delta\right\},

which is contained in
\{s\in\mathcal{S}:\sigma(s)\leq c_{0}\delta\}
with
\rho(s,t)\geq u(c_{0}\delta/2)\frac{c_{0}\delta}{2}
for different
s,t\in\mathcal{S}^{*}(\delta)
. Taking into account that

\displaystyle D\left(u\delta,\{t\in\mathcal{S}:\sigma(t)\leq\delta\},\rho\right)

\displaystyle\leq D\left(u\delta,\{t\in\mathcal{S}:\tilde{\sigma}(t)\leq c_{0}\delta\},c_{0}\tilde{\rho}\right)

\displaystyle=D\left(c_{0}^{-1}u\delta,\{t\in\mathcal{S}:\tilde{\sigma}(t)\leq c_{0}\delta\},\tilde{\rho}\right),

we consequently have from assumption (c) that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\#\mathcal{S}^{*}(\delta)&\leq D\left(\frac{1}{2c_{0}}u(c_{0}\delta/2)c_{0}\delta,\{s\in\mathcal{S}:\tilde{\sigma}(s)\leq c_{0}^{2}\delta\},\tilde{\rho}\right)\\
&\leq A_{0}\left(2c_{0}^{2}\right)^{B}c_{0}^{-2V}u(c_{0}\delta/2)^{-B}\delta^{-V}.\end{split}

(E.4)

In order to bound
\frac{|Z(t)|}{\tilde{\sigma}(t)}
for all
t\in\mathcal{S}^{*}
we define

H(t):=\sqrt{2V\log(1/\sigma(t))+2B\log(1/u(\sigma(t)))+4\log\log(e/\sigma(t))}

and choose

u(\delta):=\log(e/\delta)^{-2}.

Furthermore, we set

\hat{H}(t):=\sqrt{2V\log(1/\tilde{\sigma}(t))+(2V+4B+5)\log\log(e/\tilde{\sigma}(t))}.

With our choice of
u
and
\delta_{0}^{\prime}
small enough such that
\log\log(e/\tilde{\sigma}(t))\geq\log(c_{0})\vee\log(2)
for all
t
satisfying
\sigma(t)\leq\delta\leq\delta_{0}^{\prime}
(this choice is possible by condition (a)), we have

\displaystyle H(t)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2V\log(1/\sigma(t))+(4B+4)\log\log(e/\sigma(t))}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2V\log(c_{0}/\tilde{\sigma}(t))+(4B+4)\log\log(c_{0}e/\tilde{\sigma}(t))}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2V\log(1/\tilde{\sigma}(t))+2V\log(c_{0})+(4B+4)\log\log(c_{0}e/\tilde{\sigma}(t))}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2V\log(1/\tilde{\sigma}(t))+(2V+4B+5)\log\log(e/\tilde{\sigma}(t))}=\hat{H}(t),

where we used

\displaystyle(4B+4)\log\log(c_{0}e/\tilde{\sigma}(t))

\displaystyle=(4B+4)\log\left(\log(e/\tilde{\sigma}(t))+\log(c_{0})\right)

\displaystyle\leq(4B+4)\log\left(2\log(e/\tilde{\sigma}(t))\right)

\displaystyle=(4B+4)\log\log(e/\tilde{\sigma}(t))+(4B+4)\log(2)

\displaystyle\leq(4B+5)\log\log(e/\tilde{\sigma}(t)).

Note further that

\left\{t\in\mathcal{S}^{*}:\tilde{\sigma}(t)\leq\delta\right\}\subset\left\{t\in\mathcal{S}^{*}:\sigma(t)\leq c_{0}\delta\right\}.

We now may choose
\delta_{0}^{\prime}
even small enough such that
H(t)\geq 1
for
\sigma(t)\leq c_{0}^{-1}\delta_{0}^{\prime}
.
Then with assumption (b) we have for
0<\delta\leq\delta_{0}^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{t\in\mathcal{S}^{*}:\tilde{\sigma}(t)\leq\delta}\left(\frac{|Z(t)|}{\tilde{\sigma}(t)}-\hat{H}(t)\right)>0\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69046pt\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{t\in\mathcal{S}^{*}:\sigma(t)\leq c_{0}\delta}\left(\frac{|Z(t)|}{\tilde{\sigma}(t)}-H(t)\right)>0\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69046pt\leq\sum_{t\in\mathcal{S}^{*}:\sigma(t)\leq c_{0}\delta}\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{|Z(t)|}{\tilde{\sigma}(t)}\geq H(t)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69046pt\leq\sum_{t\in\mathcal{S}^{*}:\sigma(t)\leq c_{0}\delta}L\exp\left(-V\log(1/\sigma(t))-B\log(1/u(\sigma(t)))-2\log\log(e/\sigma(t))\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 31.2982pt\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\log\left(2V\log(1/\sigma(t))+2B\log(1/u(\sigma(t)))+4\log\log(e/\sigma(t))\right)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69046pt=L\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{S}^{*}(2^{-k}\delta)}\sigma(t)^{V}u(\sigma(t))^{B}\log(e/\sigma(t))^{-2}

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt\cdot\sqrt{2V\log(1/\sigma(t))+2B\log(1/u(\sigma(t)))+4\log\log(e/\sigma(t))}.

The choice of
u
implies
2B\log(1/u(\sigma(t)))=4B\log\log(e/\sigma(t))
and since we have additionally
\log\log(e/\sigma(t))\leq\log(e/\sigma(t))
, the square root factor is bounded by
\sqrt{2(V+2B+2)\log(e/\sigma(t))}
. We proceed by

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{t\in\mathcal{S}^{*}:\tilde{\sigma}(t)\leq\delta}\left(\frac{|Z(t)|}{\tilde{\sigma}(t)}-\hat{H}(t)\right)>0\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69046pt\leq L\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{S}^{*}(2^{-k}\delta)}\sigma(t)^{V}u(\sigma(t))^{B}\log(e/\sigma(t))^{-2}\sqrt{2(V+2B+2)\log(e/\sigma(t))}

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69046pt\leq C_{1}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{S}^{*}(2^{-k}\delta)}(2^{-k}\delta)^{V}u(2^{-k}\delta)^{B}\log(e/(2^{-k}\delta))^{-\frac{3}{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69046pt\leq C_{1}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left(\#\mathcal{S}^{*}(2^{-k}\delta)\right)(2^{-k}\delta)^{V}u(2^{-k}\delta)^{B}\left(\log(e/\delta)+\log(2)k\right)^{-\frac{3}{2}},

where
C_{1}:=L\sqrt{2(V+2B+2)}
. Now, by (
E.4
) we have

\displaystyle\left(\#\mathcal{S}^{*}(2^{-k}\delta)\right)(2^{-k}\delta)^{V}u(2^{-k}\delta)^{B}

\displaystyle\leq A_{0}2^{B}c_{0}^{2B-2V}\left(\sup_{0<x\leq 1}\frac{u(x)}{u(c_{0}x/2)}\right)^{B}

\displaystyle\leq A_{0}2^{B}c_{0}^{2B-2V}\log(2e/c_{0})^{2B}=:C_{2}.

Here we used that

\frac{u(x)}{u(c_{0}x/2)}=\left(\frac{\log(2e/(c_{0}x))}{\log(e/x)}\right)^{2}=\left(\frac{\log(2/c_{0})}{\log(e/x)}+1\right)^{2},

which is increasing for growing
x
and equals
\log(2e/c_{0})
for
x=1
. By the integral test for convergence,

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(\log(e/\delta)+\log(2)k)^{-\frac{3}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\log(e/\delta)^{-\frac{2}{3}}+\int_{0}^{\infty}(\log(e/\delta)+\log(2)x)^{-\frac{3}{2}}dx

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+\frac{2}{\log(2)}\right)\log(e/\delta)^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

Finally, for
C_{3}=C_{1}C_{2}(1+2/\log(2))
and
\delta\leq\delta_{0}^{\prime}
we have shown so far

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{t\in\mathcal{S}^{*}:\tilde{\sigma}(t)\leq\delta}\left(\frac{|Z(t)|}{\tilde{\sigma}(t)}-\hat{H}(t)\right)>0\right)\leq C_{3}\log(e/\delta)^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

(E.5)

To proceed, let
s\in\mathcal{S}
be arbitrary and choose
t\in\mathcal{S}^{*}
to satisfy
\sigma(s)\leq\sigma(t)
and
\rho(s,t)<\log(e/\sigma(s))^{-2}\sigma(s)
, which is possible by (
E.3
) and our specific choice of
u
. Then

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\tilde{\sigma}(t)}{\tilde{\sigma}(s)}-1\right|\leq\frac{\tilde{\rho}(s,t)}{\tilde{\sigma}(s)}<c_{0}^{2}\log(e/(c_{0}\tilde{\sigma}(s))^{-2},

(E.6)

where we used (
E.1
) for the first inequality and

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}(s,t)\leq c_{0}\rho(s,t)

\displaystyle<c_{0}\log(e/\sigma(s))^{-2}\sigma(s)

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}\log(e/(c_{0}\tilde{\sigma}(s)))^{-2}c_{0}\tilde{\sigma}(s)

for the second.
Now we have on the set
\mathcal{A}(Q,2\tilde{\sigma}(s))
,

\displaystyle\frac{|Z(s)|}{\tilde{\sigma}(s)}-\frac{|Z(t)|}{\tilde{\sigma}(t)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|Z(s)-Z(t)|}{\tilde{\sigma}(s)}+\frac{|Z(t)|}{\tilde{\sigma}(t)}\left|\frac{\tilde{\sigma}(t)}{\tilde{\sigma}(s)}-1\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{Q\tilde{\rho}(s,t)\log(e/\tilde{\rho}(s,t))}{\tilde{\sigma}(s)}+\frac{|Z(t)|}{\tilde{\sigma}(t)}\frac{\tilde{\rho}(s,t)}{\tilde{\sigma}(s)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{4}\log(e/(C_{5}\tilde{\sigma}(s)))^{-1}+\frac{|Z(t)|}{\tilde{\sigma}(t)}c_{0}^{2}\log(e/(c_{0}\tilde{\sigma}(s)))^{-2}

for suitable constants
C_{4}=C_{4}(Q,c_{0}),C_{5}=C_{5}(c_{0})
. Using that
x\log(e/x)
is increasing on
(0,1]
, this follows from the estimate

\displaystyle\frac{Q\tilde{\rho}(s,t)}{\tilde{\sigma}(s)}\log(e/\tilde{\rho}(s,t))

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\leq c_{0}^{2}\log(e/(c_{0}\tilde{\sigma}(s)))^{-2}\log\left(e/(c_{0}^{2}\log(e/(c_{0}\tilde{\sigma}(s))^{-2}\tilde{\sigma}(s))\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=c_{0}^{2}\log(e/(c_{0}\tilde{\sigma}(s)))^{-2}\left(\log\left(e/(c_{0}^{2}\tilde{\sigma}(s)\right)+2\log\log\left(e/(c_{0}\tilde{\sigma}(s)\right)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\leq C_{4}\log(e/(C_{5}\tilde{\sigma}(s)))^{-1}.

Consequently, if in addition
\frac{|Z(t)|}{\tilde{\sigma}(t)}\leq\hat{H}(t)
, then

\displaystyle\frac{|Z(s)|}{\tilde{\sigma}(s)}

\displaystyle\leq\hat{H}(t)+C_{4}\log(e/C_{5}\tilde{\sigma}(s))^{-1}+\hat{H}(t)c_{0}^{2}\log(e/(c_{0}\tilde{\sigma}(s))^{-2}

Now we use that by choice of
s,t
and assumption (a) we have

\tilde{\sigma}(t)\geq c_{0}^{-1}\sigma(t)\geq c_{0}^{-1}\sigma(s)\geq c_{0}^{-2}\tilde{\sigma}(s)

and with this inequality

\displaystyle\hat{H}(t)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2V\log(1/(c_{0}^{-2}\tilde{\sigma}(s)))+(2V+4B+4)\log\log(e/(c_{0}^{-2}\tilde{\sigma}(s)))}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2V\log(1/\tilde{\sigma}(s))+C_{6}\log\log(e/\tilde{\sigma}(s))},

with a constant
C_{6}=C_{6}(V,B,c_{0})
. Using this and
\sqrt{x}\leq 1+x
we find

\displaystyle\frac{|Z(s)|}{\tilde{\sigma}(s)}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2V\log(1/\tilde{\sigma}(s))+C_{6}\log\log(e/\tilde{\sigma}(s))}+C_{4}\log(e/C_{5}\tilde{\sigma}(s))^{-1}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\left(1+2V\log(1/\tilde{\sigma}(s))+C_{6}\log\log(e/\tilde{\sigma}(s))\right)c_{0}^{2}\log(e/(c_{0}\tilde{\sigma}(s)))^{-2}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2V\log(1/\tilde{\sigma}(s))+C_{6}\log\log(e/\tilde{\sigma}(s))}+C_{7}\log(e/(C_{8}\tilde{\sigma}(s)))^{-1}

for suitable constants
C_{7}=C_{7}(V,B,Q,c_{0})
and
C_{8}=C_{8}(c_{0})
.
Finally, note that
\tilde{\sigma}(s)\leq\delta
implies
\tilde{\sigma}(t)\leq c_{0}^{2}\delta
because

\tilde{\sigma}(t)\leq c_{0}\sigma(t)\leq c_{0}\left(\sigma(s)+\rho(s,t)\right)\leq 2c_{0}\sigma(s)\leq 2c_{0}^{2}\tilde{\sigma}(s),

where we used
\rho(s,t)\leq\sigma(s)
by choice of
t
. Consequently, on
\{\tilde{\sigma}(s)\leq\delta\}
the ratio
\frac{|Z(s)|}{\tilde{\sigma}(s)}
is not greater than

\sqrt{2V\log(1/\tilde{\sigma}(s))+C_{6}\log\log(e/\tilde{\sigma}(s))}+C_{7}\log(e/(C_{8}\tilde{\sigma}(s)))^{-1}

with probability at least
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}(Q,2\delta))-C_{3}\log(e/(c_{0}^{2}\delta))^{-\frac{1}{2}}
by (
E.5
) for every
\delta\leq c_{0}^{-2}\delta_{0}^{\prime}
. This yields the statement of the theorem if we take a suitable
C_{0}=C_{0}(A_{0},B,V,Q,c_{0})
and set
\delta_{0}:=c_{0}^{-2}\delta_{0}^{\prime}
.
∎

Next, we consider a family
(Z_{T})_{T\geq 0}
of stochastic processes, where for each
T\geq 0
,
Z_{T}=(Z_{T}(s))_{s\in\mathcal{S}}
is a stochastic process on some totally bounded countable semimetric space
(\mathcal{S},\rho_{T})
, where
\rho_{T}:\Omega\times\mathcal{S}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
itself is allowed to be random. Moreover, there exists a spread measure
\sigma_{T}:\Omega\times\mathcal{S}\rightarrow(0,1]
for
Z_{T}
such that
\sigma_{T}
and
\rho_{T}
satisfy (
E.1
) and (
E.2
) almost surely.

Theorem E.4
.

Let
\sigma_{T}
,
\rho_{T}
be as above together with deterministic metric
\rho
and
\sigma
with
(
E.1
)
and
(
E.2
)
. Suppose there exists a family of random sets
(\mathcal{C}_{T})_{T\geq 0}
with
\lim_{T\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{C}_{T})=1
such that the following three conditions are satisfied:

(a)

There exists a constant
c_{0}>0
such that on
\mathcal{C}_{T}
we have

\frac{1}{c_{0}}\leq\frac{\sigma_{T}}{\sigma}\leq c_{0}\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{1}{c_{0}}\leq\frac{\rho_{T}}{\rho}\leq c_{0}.

(b)

For arbitrary
s\in\mathcal{S}
and
\lambda\geq 1
there exists a constant
L>0
such that

\mathbb{P}\left(\{|Z_{T}(s)|\geq\sigma_{T}(s)\lambda\}\cap\mathcal{C}_{T}\right)\leq L\exp\left(-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}+\log(\lambda)\right).

(c)

There exists a constant
L^{\prime}\geq 1
such that for arbitrary
s,t\in\mathcal{S}
and
\lambda\geq 1
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\{|Z_{T}(s)-Z_{T}(t)|\geq\rho_{T}(s,t)\lambda\}\cap\mathcal{C}_{T}\right)\leq L^{\prime}\exp\left(-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}+\log(\lambda)\right).

(d)

For some constants
A_{0},B,V>0
we have the following bound for the capacity numbers on
\mathcal{C}_{T}
:

D\left(u\delta,\{s\in\mathcal{S}:\sigma_{T}(s)\leq\delta\},\rho_{T}\right)\leq A_{0}u^{-B}\delta^{-V}\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{ for all }u,\delta\in(0,1].

Then for every
\epsilon>0
there exists
T_{\epsilon}
and
\lambda_{\epsilon}>0
such that for
T\geq T_{\epsilon}
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\frac{|Z_{T}(s)|/\sigma_{T}(s)-\sqrt{2V\log(1/\sigma_{T}(s))}}{D(\sigma_{T}(s))}\geq\lambda_{\epsilon}\right)\leq\epsilon,

where
D(\delta):=\log(e/\delta)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\log\log(e^{e}/\delta)
. Moreover, the sequence of random variables

\left(\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\frac{|Z_{T}(s)|/\sigma_{T}(s)-\sqrt{2V\log(1/\sigma_{T}(s))}}{D(\sigma_{T}(s))}\right)_{T\geq 0}

is asymptotically tight, provided that for some
T_{0}>0
,

\displaystyle\inf_{T\geq T_{0}}\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left(\sigma_{T}(s)|_{\mathcal{C}_{T}}\right)>0.

(E.7)

First of all, we define the set

\mathcal{A}_{T}(Q,\delta):=\left\{\sup_{s,t\in\mathcal{S}:\rho_{T}(s,t)\leq\delta}\frac{|Z_{T}(t)-Z_{T}(s)|}{\rho_{T}(s,t)\log(e/\rho_{T}(s,t))}\leq Q\right\}.

Because
x\log(e/x)
is monotonously increasing on
(0,1]
, we have by assumption (a) that

\mathcal{A}\left(Q,c_{0}\delta\right)\subset\mathcal{A}_{T}(Q,\delta)

where

\mathcal{A}\left(Q,\delta\right):=\left\{\sup_{s,t\in\mathcal{S}:\rho(s,t)\leq\delta}\frac{|Z_{T}(t)-Z_{T}(s)|}{c_{0}^{-1}\rho(s,t)\log(e/(c_{0}^{-1}\rho(s,t)))}\leq Q\right\}.

Again by assumption (a) we find that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\{|Z_{T}(s)-Z_{T}(t)|\geq\rho(s,t)\lambda\}\cap\mathcal{C}_{T}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\{|Z_{T}(s)-Z_{T}(t)|\geq c_{0}^{-1}\rho_{T}(s,t)\lambda\}\cap\mathcal{C}_{T}\right),

which implies together with the sub-Gaussian bound of assumption (c) that the process
Z_{T}
with semimetric
\rho(\cdot,\cdot)
satisfies assumption (a) of Proposition
E.1
for a suitable constant
L^{\prime\prime}
.
Now, note the following: For
\tilde{q},q>0
and
G(\lambda,\delta)=\tilde{q}\lambda^{q}
, Remark
3
in the technical report
[
20
]
entails that under assumption (d) for
\delta=1
,

J(\epsilon,1)\leq C^{\prime}\epsilon\log(e/\epsilon)^{q}\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{ for all }0<\epsilon\leq 1,

for
C^{\prime}=\tilde{q}\max(1+2B,\log(A_{0}^{2}))^{q}\int_{0}^{1}\log(e/z)^{q}dz
and
J(\cdot,\cdot)
given in Proposition
E.1
.
Then, with
\tilde{q}=q=1
Proposition
E.1
implies for
Q=1+12C^{\prime}
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{A}_{T}(Q,\delta)^{c}\cap\mathcal{C}_{T}\right)\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{A}(Q,c_{0}\delta)^{c}\right)\leq\frac{L^{\prime\prime}c_{0}\delta}{2}.

(E.8)

For the rest of the proof define

\Psi_{T}(s):=\frac{|Z_{T}(s)|/\sigma_{T}(s)-\sqrt{2V\log(1/\sigma_{T}(s))}}{D(\sigma_{T}(s))}.

Next, we bound from above,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\Psi_{T}(s)\geq\lambda\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\left\{\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\Psi_{T}(s)\geq\lambda\right\}\cap\mathcal{C}_{T}\right)+\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{C}_{T}^{c}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\left\{\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}:\sigma_{T}(s)<\delta}\Psi_{T}(s)\geq\lambda\right\}\cap\mathcal{C}_{T}\right)+\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{C}_{T}^{c}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\mathbb{P}\left(\left\{\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}:\sigma_{T}(s)\geq\delta}\Psi_{T}(s)\geq\lambda\right\}\cap\mathcal{C}_{T}\right)

for some
0<\delta<1
. Let
\epsilon>0
. We are now going to show that for appropriate
\delta=\delta_{\epsilon}>0
there exists
T_{\epsilon}
and
\lambda_{\epsilon}
such that each summand is
<\frac{\epsilon}{3}
for
T>T_{\epsilon}
and
\lambda=\lambda_{\epsilon}
.

(i)

We have

\displaystyle\sqrt{2V\log(1/\delta)+C_{0}\log\log(e/\delta)}+C_{0}\log(e/(C_{0}\delta))^{-1}-\sqrt{2V\log(1/\delta)}

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69046pt=\frac{C_{0}\log\log(e/\delta)}{\sqrt{2V\log(1/\delta)+C_{0}\log\log(e/\delta)}+\sqrt{2V\log(1/\delta)}}+\frac{C_{0}}{\log(e/(C_{0}\delta))}

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69046pt=\mathcal{O}\left(\log\log(e/\delta)\log(e/\delta)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right)

for
\delta\searrow 0
. Conditions (a), (b) and (d) are clearly the same as those of Theorem
E.2
and we may apply it to get for
\lambda
large enough,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left\{\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}:\sigma_{T}(s)<\delta}\Psi_{T}(s)\geq\lambda\right\}\cap\mathcal{C}_{T}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq 1-\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}_{T}(Q,2\delta)\cap\mathcal{C}_{T})+C_{0}\log(e/(2c_{0}^{2}\delta))^{-\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}_{T}(Q,2\delta)^{c}\cap\mathcal{C}_{T})+\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{C}_{T}^{c}\right)+C_{0}\log(e/(2c_{0}^{2}\delta))^{-\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq L^{\prime\prime}c_{0}\delta+\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{C}_{T}^{c}\right)+C_{0}\log(e/(2c_{0}^{2}\delta))^{-\frac{1}{2}},

where we used

\displaystyle 1-\mathbb{P}(A\cap B)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}((A\cap B)^{c})=\mathbb{P}(A^{c}\cup B^{c})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}((A^{c}\cap B)\cup B^{c})\leq\mathbb{P}(A^{c}\cap B)+\mathbb{P}(B^{c})

in the second step and (
E.8
) in the last one. Now choose
\delta_{\epsilon}>0
fullfilling
L^{\prime\prime}c_{0}\delta_{\epsilon}+C_{0}\log(e/(2c_{0}^{2}\delta_{\epsilon}))^{-\frac{1}{2}}<\frac{\epsilon}{6}
and
T_{2}
large enough such that
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{C}_{T}^{c})<\frac{\epsilon}{6}
for
T\geq T_{2}
.

(ii)

It is clear by
\lim_{T\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{C}_{T})=1
that there exists
T_{1}
such that for all
T\geq T_{1}
we have
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{C}_{T}^{c})<\frac{\epsilon}{3}
.

(iii)

Lastly, we need to bound the remaining probability by
\frac{\epsilon}{3}
where we have to use
\delta=\delta_{\epsilon}
from step (i). On
\{\sigma_{T}(s)\geq\delta\}
we have the bound

\displaystyle\Psi_{T}(s)\leq\frac{C^{\prime\prime}}{\delta}\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}|Z_{T}(s)|,

(E.9)

where the constant
C^{\prime\prime}
emerges from minimizing
D(\cdot)
on
[\delta,1]
.
Now fix
T
and choose
\kappa>0
together with a maximal subset
\{s_{1},\dots,s_{N}\}\subset\mathcal{S}
that fullfills
\rho(s_{i},s_{j})>\kappa/c_{0}
for all
i\neq j
. From assumptions (a) and (d) we know that
N\leq A_{0}c_{0}^{2B}\kappa^{-B}
. Now, on the set
\mathcal{A}_{T}(Q,\kappa)
,

\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}|Z_{T}(s)|\leq Q\kappa\log(e/\kappa)+\max_{i=1,\dots,N}|Z_{T}(s_{i})|.

Hence, by the union bound and assumption (a) we then have for
\lambda^{\prime}:=\lambda\delta/C^{\prime\prime}
with
\lambda
large enough and
\kappa
small enough to ensure
\lambda^{\prime}-Q\kappa\log(e/\kappa)\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left\{\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}|Z_{T}(s)|>\lambda^{\prime}\right\}\cap\mathcal{C}_{T}\cap\mathcal{A}_{T}(Q,\kappa)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 19.91684pt\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\left\{Q\kappa\log(e/\kappa)+\max_{i=1,\dots,N}|Z_{T}(s_{i})|>\lambda^{\prime}\right\}\cap\mathcal{C}_{T}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 19.91684pt\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\left\{\max_{i=1,\dots,N}|Z_{T}(s_{i})|>\lambda^{\prime}-Q\kappa\log(e/\kappa)\right\}\cap\mathcal{C}_{T}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 19.91684pt\leq\exp\left(\log(N)-\frac{(\lambda^{\prime}-Q\kappa\log(e/\kappa))^{2}}{2}+\log(\lambda^{\prime}-Q\kappa\log(e/\kappa))\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 19.91684pt=\exp\left(\log\left(A_{0}c_{0}^{2B}\right)-B\log(\kappa)-\frac{(\lambda^{\prime}-Q\kappa\log(e/\kappa))^{2}}{2}\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt\left.{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\frac{1}{2}}+\log(\lambda^{\prime}-Q\kappa\log(e/\kappa))\right).

Thus, with (
E.8
) and (
E.9
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left\{\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}:\sigma_{T}(s)\geq\delta}\Psi_{T}(s)>\lambda\right\}\cap\mathcal{C}_{T}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\left\{\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}|Z_{T}(s)|>\lambda^{\prime}\right\}\cap\mathcal{A}_{T}(Q,\kappa)\cap\mathcal{C}_{T}\right)+\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{A}_{T}(Q,\kappa)^{c}\cap\mathcal{C}_{T}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\leq\exp\left(\log\left(A_{0}c_{0}^{2B}\right)-B\log(\kappa)-\frac{1}{2}(\lambda^{\prime}-Q\kappa\log(e/\kappa))^{2}\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 156.49014pt\left.{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\frac{1}{2}}+\log(\lambda^{\prime}-Q\kappa\log(e/\kappa))\right)+\frac{L^{\prime\prime}\kappa}{2}.

Note that we accomplished to bound the probability independent of
T
. Now, choose
\kappa_{\epsilon}
small enough such that
\frac{L^{\prime\prime}\kappa_{\epsilon}}{2}<\frac{\epsilon}{6}
and after that
\lambda_{\epsilon}
large enough in order to ensure

\displaystyle\exp\left(\log\left(A_{0}c_{0}^{2B}\right)-B\log(\kappa_{\epsilon})-\frac{(\lambda_{\epsilon}\delta/C-Q\kappa_{\epsilon}\log(e/\kappa_{\epsilon}))^{2}}{2}\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\left.{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\frac{1}{2}}+\log(\lambda_{\epsilon}\delta/C-Q\kappa_{\epsilon}\log(e/\kappa_{\epsilon}))\right)<\frac{\epsilon}{6}.

For those choices we then have

\mathbb{P}\left(\left\{\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}:\sigma_{T}(s)\geq\delta}\Psi_{T}(s)>\lambda\right\}\cap\mathcal{C}_{T}\right)\leq\frac{\epsilon}{3}.

In conclusion, for all
T>\max\{T_{1},T_{2}\}
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\Psi_{T}(s)>\lambda_{\epsilon}\right)<\epsilon,

which gives the first claim of the theorem. For the asymptotic tightness, it remains to show that for any
\epsilon>0
there exists
\lambda_{\epsilon}^{\prime}>0
and
T_{\epsilon}^{\prime}
such that for
T\geq T_{\epsilon}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\Psi_{T}(s)<-\lambda_{\epsilon}^{\prime}\right)\leq\epsilon.

(E.10)

By non-negativity of
|Z_{T}(s)|/\sigma_{T}(s)
and
D(\cdot)
, it is enough to show that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(-\inf_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\sqrt{2V\log(1/\sigma_{T}(s))}<-\lambda_{\epsilon}^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq\mathbb{P}\left(-\inf_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\sqrt{2V\log(1/\sigma_{T}(s))}<-\lambda_{\epsilon}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}_{T}\right)+\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{C}_{T}^{c}\right)

is bounded from above by
\epsilon
for
T\geq T_{\epsilon}^{\prime}
. The second term is bounded by
\frac{\epsilon}{2}
for
T\geq T_{1}^{\prime}
large enough by assumption on
\mathcal{C}_{T}
. For the first one, this bound follows for
T\geq T_{0}
directly from the assumption (
E.7
) and
\lambda_{\epsilon}^{\prime}
large enough. Now (
E.10
) follows for
T\geq T_{\epsilon}^{\prime}:=T_{1}^{\prime}\vee T_{0}
.
∎

E.2
Proof of Theorem
3.1

The proof of Theorem
3.1
will be an application of Theorem
E.4
. One major part is establishing the exponential inequalities on appropriate sets - the other bounding the covering numbers. Our proof of the first part relies substantially on the following result.

Proposition E.5
(
[
49
]
, Proposition A.1).

Let
(M_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be a continuous martingale with
M_{0}=0
. Then for every
T>0
,
\theta>0
,
S\geq 1
and
\lambda\geq 1
we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\left|M_{T}\right|>\lambda\sqrt{\langle M\rangle_{T}},\theta\leq\sqrt{\langle M\rangle_{T}}\leq\theta S\right)\leq 4\lambda\sqrt{e}(1+\log(S))\exp\left(-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\right).

The proof is an application of Theorem
E.4
for the process

Z_{T}(y,h):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}\hat{\sigma}_{T,\max}}\int_{0}^{T}K_{y,h}(X_{s})dW_{s}

and the set

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{T}}:=\left\{(y,h)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\mid 0<h\leq A\textrm{ and }-A+h\leq y\leq A-h\right\}

(E.11)

with dense subset
\mathcal{T}=\overline{\mathcal{T}}\cap\mathbb{Q}^{2}
. The (random) spread measure of
Z_{T}
is given by
\overline{\sigma}_{T}(y,h):=\frac{\hat{\sigma}_{T}(y,h)}{\hat{\sigma}_{T,\max}}\leq 1
and the corresponding semimetric by

\overline{\rho}_{T}((y,h),(y^{\prime},h^{\prime}))^{2}:=\frac{1}{T\hat{\sigma}_{T,\max}^{2}}\int_{0}^{T}\left(K_{y,h}(X_{s})-K_{y^{\prime},h^{\prime}}(X_{s})\right)^{2}ds.

The random map
\hat{\sigma}_{T}^{2}:\overline{\mathcal{T}}\rightarrow(0,1]
is continuous, as

\left|\hat{\sigma}_{T}(y_{n},h_{n})^{2}-\hat{\sigma}_{T}(y,h)^{2}\right|\leq\left\|K_{y_{n},h_{n}}^{2}-K_{y,h}^{2}\right\|_{[-A,A]}

and the right-hand side converges to zero for
(y_{n},h_{n})\to(y,h)
. In particular, it follows that
\overline{\sigma}_{T}
is continuous in
(y,h)
and hence the level sets in (
E.2
) are compact because they are bounded and closed as the preimage of the closed set
[\delta,1]
. The inequality
|\overline{\sigma}_{T}(y,h)-\overline{\sigma}_{T}(y^{\prime},h^{\prime})|\leq\overline{\rho}_{T}((y,h),(y^{\prime},h^{\prime}))
follows by Cauchy-Schwarz’ inequality.
Moreover, for the application of Theorem
E.4
, we define the set

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{T,b_{0}}:=\left\{\sup_{z\in[-A,A]}\left|\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}T}L_{T}^{z}(X)-q_{b_{0}}(z)\right|<\frac{1}{2}L_{*}\right\}

where
L_{*}
is the lower bound of the invariant density given in Lemma
D.1
. By (
D.1
) we have
\lim_{T\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{C}_{T,b_{0}})=1
and on
\mathcal{C}_{T,b_{0}}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}L_{*}\leq\frac{1}{2}q_{b_{0}}(z)\leq\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}T}L_{T}^{z}(X)\leq\frac{3}{2}q_{b_{0}}(z)\leq\frac{3}{2}L^{*}

(E.12)

with the upper bound
L^{*}
from Lemma
D.1
. Now we check each of the conditions (a)-(d) of Theorem
E.4
and define the deterministic counterparts

\overline{\sigma}_{b}(y,h)^{2}:=\sigma_{b,\max}^{-2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{y,h}(z)^{2}q_{b}(z)dz

and

\overline{\rho}_{b}((y,h),(y^{\prime},h^{\prime}))^{2}:=\sigma_{b,\max}^{-2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(K_{y,h}(z)-K_{y^{\prime},h^{\prime}}(z)\right)^{2}q_{b}(z)dz,

of
\sigma_{T}
and
\rho_{T}
, where in both cases
\sigma_{b,\max}^{2}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbbm{1}_{[-A,A]}(z)q_{b}(z)dz
. The validity of (
E.1
) and compactness of (
E.2
) are checked in the same way as for the random metrics.

(a)

Using the occupation times formula, we have

\hat{\sigma}_{T,\max}^{2}=\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbbm{1}_{[-A,A]}(X_{s})ds=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbbm{1}_{[-A,A]}(z)\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}T}L_{T}^{z}(X)dz.

Hence, on
\mathcal{C}_{T,b_{0}}
we have by (
E.12
)

\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{b,\max}^{2}\leq\hat{\sigma}_{T,\max}^{2}\leq\frac{3}{2}\sigma_{b,\max}^{2}.

In the same way we see

\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{b,\max}^{2}\overline{\sigma}_{b}(y,h)^{2}\leq\hat{\sigma}_{T}(y,h)^{2}\leq\frac{3}{2}\sigma_{b,\max}^{2}\overline{\sigma}_{b}(y,h)^{2}

and (omitting the argument
((y,h),(y^{\prime},h^{\prime}))
)

\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{b,\max}^{2}\overline{\rho}_{b}^{2}\leq\hat{\rho}_{T}^{2}\leq\frac{3}{2}\sigma_{b,\max}^{2}\overline{\rho}_{b}^{2}.

Combining these estimates yields assumption (a) of Theorem
E.4
for the quotients
\overline{\sigma}_{T}/\overline{\sigma}_{b}
and
\overline{\rho}_{T}/\overline{\rho}_{b}
with
c_{0}=\sqrt{3}
.

(b)

By the occupation times formula and (
E.12
) we have on
\mathcal{C}_{T,b_{0}}

\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}L_{*}}\|K_{y,h}\|_{L^{2}}\leq\hat{\sigma}_{T}(y,h)\leq\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}L^{*}}\|K_{y,h}\|_{L^{2}}.

Note furthermore that
Z^{\prime}_{t}(y,h)=\hat{\sigma}_{t,\max}\sqrt{t}Z_{t}(y,h)
is a martingale (as a process indexed in
t
) with quadratic variation
\langle Z^{\prime}(y,h)\rangle_{t}=t\hat{\sigma}_{t}(y,h)^{2}
. Setting
\theta=\sqrt{TL_{*}/2}\|K_{y,h}\|_{L^{2}}
and
S=\sqrt{3L^{*}/L_{*}}\geq 1
in Proposition
E.5
gives for
\lambda\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(|Z_{T}(y,h)|\geq\lambda\overline{\sigma}_{T}(y,h),\mathcal{C}_{T,b_{0}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=\mathbb{P}\left(|Z^{\prime}_{T}(y,h)|\geq\lambda\sqrt{T}\hat{\sigma}_{T}(y,h),\mathcal{C}_{T,b_{0}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\leq 4\sqrt{e}\left(1+\log\big(\sqrt{3L^{*}/L_{*}}\big)\right)\exp\left(-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}+\log(\lambda)\right).

(c)

By the same arguments as in part (b) for
\lambda\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(|\tilde{Z}_{T}|>\lambda\overline{\rho}_{T}((y,h),(y^{\prime},h^{\prime})),\mathcal{C}_{T,b_{0}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\leq 4\sqrt{e}\left(1+\log\big(\sqrt{3L^{*}/L_{*}}\big)\right)\exp\left(-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}+\log(\lambda)\right),

by an application of Proposition
E.5
. The sub-exponential tail follows then for
L^{\prime}>0
large enough because
\exp\left(-\lambda^{2}/2+\log(\lambda)+\lambda\right)
is bounded from above for
\lambda\geq 1
.

(d)

We split the interval
[-A,A]
into a partition
(M_{k})_{1\leq k\leq N}
such that

\lambda(M_{k})\leq\frac{\hat{\sigma}_{T,\max}^{2}}{6L^{*}\|K\|_{TV}}(u\delta)^{2}

for
1\leq k\leq N
, where equality holds for
k\neq N
. Here and subsequently,
\|K\|_{TV}
denotes the total variation of
K
and
\lambda(\cdot)
the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}
.
Then we have

2A=\lambda([-A,A])=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\lambda(M_{k})\geq(N-1)\frac{\hat{\sigma}_{T,\max}^{2}}{6L^{*}\|K\|_{TV}}(u\delta)^{2},

or equivalently

\displaystyle N\leq 1+\frac{12AL^{*}\|K\|_{TV}}{\hat{\sigma}_{T,\max}^{2}}(u\delta)^{-2}.

(E.13)

Take
(y,h)\in\overline{\mathcal{T}}
such that
\overline{\sigma}_{T}(y,h)\leq\delta
, i.e.

\frac{1}{T\hat{\sigma}_{T,\max}^{2}}\int_{0}^{T}K_{y,h}(X_{s})^{2}ds=\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}_{T,\max}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{y,h}(z)^{2}\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}T}L_{T}^{z}(X)dz\leq\delta^{2}

by the occupation times formula. Now using the lower bound (
E.12
) for the averaged local time on
\mathcal{C}_{T,b_{0}}
we get

\delta^{2}\geq\frac{L_{*}}{2\hat{\sigma}_{T,\max}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{y,h}(z)^{2}dz=\frac{hL_{*}\|K\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}{2\hat{\sigma}_{T,\max}^{2}},

which implies

h\leq 2\hat{\sigma}_{T,\max}^{2}\delta^{2}(L_{*}\|K\|_{L^{2}}^{2})^{-1}.

Now suppose
y-h\in M_{i}
and
y+h\in M_{j}
. As we know the length of the intervals
M_{k}
, we conclude

(i-j-1)\frac{\hat{\sigma}_{T,\max}^{2}(u\delta)^{2}}{6L^{*}\|K\|_{TV}}\leq\frac{4\delta^{2}\hat{\sigma}_{T,\max}^{2}}{L_{*}\|K\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}\hskip 10.00002pt\Leftrightarrow\hskip 10.00002pti-j\leq 1+\frac{24L^{*}\|K\|_{TV}}{L_{*}\|K\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}u^{-2}.

From this and (
E.13
), we conclude that there are at most

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left(1+\frac{24L^{*}\|K\|_{TV}}{L_{*}\|K\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}u^{-2}\right)\left(1+\frac{12AL^{*}\|K\|_{TV}}{\hat{\sigma}_{T,\max}^{2}}(u\delta)^{-2}\right)\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt\leq\left(1+12L^{*}\|K\|_{TV}\left(\frac{1}{L_{*}}+\frac{2}{L_{*}\|K\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}+\frac{24L^{*}\|K\|_{TV}}{(L_{*})^{2}\|K\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}\right)\right)u^{-4}\delta^{-2}\end{split}

(E.14)

such pairs
(i,j)
, where we used the lower bound
\hat{\sigma}_{T,\max}^{2}\geq AL_{*}
on
\mathcal{C}_{T,b_{0}}
. Let
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\subset\overline{\mathcal{T}}
be a maximal subset of
\{(y,h)\in\overline{\mathcal{T}}:\overline{\sigma}_{T}(y,h)\leq\delta\}
with
\overline{\rho}_{T}((y,h),(y^{\prime},h^{\prime}))>u\delta
for arbitrary
(y,h),(y^{\prime},h^{\prime})\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
.
Then, the proof is finished by (
E.14
) if we could show that for all
(i,j)
there is at most one such point
(y_{ij},h_{ij})
in
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
with
y_{ij}-h_{ij}\in M_{i}
and
y_{ij}+h_{ij}\in M_{j}
. To this aim, we pick
(y,h),(y^{\prime},h^{\prime})\in\overline{\mathcal{T}}
such that
y-h,y^{\prime}-h^{\prime}\in M_{i}
and
y+h,y^{\prime}+h^{\prime}\in M_{j}
and are done if we can show that
\overline{\rho}_{T}((y,h),(y^{\prime},h^{\prime}))^{2}\leq(u\delta)^{2}
.

As the kernel
K
is of bounded variation, there exists a probability measure
\mu
on
[-1,1]
and a measurable function
g
with
|g|\leq\|K\|_{TV}
such that for almost all
x\in[-1,1]
we have

K(x)=\int_{-1}^{x}gd\mu.

Since
K
is bounded by one, we have on
\mathcal{C}_{T,b_{0}}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\left(K_{y,h}(X_{s})-K_{y^{\prime},h^{\prime}}(X_{s})\right)^{2}ds

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\leq\frac{2}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\left|K_{y,h}(X_{s})-K_{y^{\prime},h^{\prime}}(X_{s})\right|ds

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt=2\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|K\left(\frac{z-y}{h}\right)-K\left(\frac{z-y^{\prime}}{h^{\prime}}\right)\right|\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}T}L_{T}^{z}(X)dz

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\leq 3L^{*}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|\int_{-1}^{\frac{z-y}{h}}g(u)d\mu(u)-\int_{-1}^{\frac{z-y^{\prime}}{h^{\prime}}}g(u)d\mu(u)\right|dz

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\leq 3L^{*}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\min\left\{\frac{z-y}{h},\frac{z-y^{\prime}}{h^{\prime}}\right\}}^{\max\left\{\frac{z-y}{h},\frac{z-y^{\prime}}{h^{\prime}}\right\}}|g(u)|d\mu(u)dz

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\leq 3L^{*}\|K\|_{TV}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{-1}^{1}\mathbbm{1}_{B_{z}((y,h),(y^{\prime},h^{\prime}))}(u)d\mu(u)dz

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt=3L^{*}\|K\|_{TV}\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbbm{1}_{B_{z}((y,h),(y^{\prime},h^{\prime}))}(u)dzd\mu(u),

with

B_{z}((y,h),(y^{\prime},h^{\prime}))=\left[\min\left\{\frac{z-y}{h},\frac{z-y^{\prime}}{h^{\prime}}\right\},\max\left\{\frac{z-y}{h},\frac{z-y^{\prime}}{h^{\prime}}\right\}\right]

and an application of Fubini’s theorem in the last step. To proceed, note that
u\in B_{z}((y,h),(y^{\prime},h^{\prime}))
if and only if

\frac{z-y}{h}\leq u\leq\frac{z-y^{\prime}}{h^{\prime}}\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{ or }\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{z-y^{\prime}}{h^{\prime}}\leq u\leq\frac{z-y}{h}.

The first condition is equivalent to
uh^{\prime}+y^{\prime}\leq z\leq uh+y
, the second one to
uh+y\leq z\leq uh^{\prime}+y^{\prime}
. Thus,

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbbm{1}_{B_{z}((y,h),(y^{\prime},h^{\prime}))}(u)dz=|y^{\prime}+uh^{\prime}-y-uh|

and we get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\left(K_{y,h}(X_{s})-K_{y^{\prime},h^{\prime}}(X_{s})\right)^{2}ds

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq 3L^{*}\|K\|_{TV}\int_{-1}^{1}|y^{\prime}+uh^{\prime}-y-uh|d\mu(u)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq 3L^{*}\|K\|_{TV}\max_{u=\pm 1}|y-y^{\prime}+u(h-h^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq 3L^{*}\|K\|_{TV}\lambda([y-h,y+h]\bigtriangleup[y^{\prime}-h^{\prime},y^{\prime}+h^{\prime}]),

where the second inequality follows from the fact, that
|y-y^{\prime}+u(h-h^{\prime})|
is maximized for
u=\textrm{sign}(y-y^{\prime})\textrm{sign}(h-h^{\prime})
. Here,
\bigtriangleup
denotes the symmetric difference of two sets.

As we chose
(y,h),(y^{\prime},h^{\prime})\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
to fullfill
y-h,y^{\prime}-h^{\prime}\in M_{i}
and
y+h,y^{\prime}+h^{\prime}\in M_{j}
, we know that

\lambda([y-h,y+h]\bigtriangleup[y^{\prime}-h^{\prime},y^{\prime}+h^{\prime}])\leq 2\frac{(u\delta)^{2}\hat{\sigma}_{T,\max}^{2}}{6L^{*}\|K\|_{TV}}.

Hence,

\displaystyle\overline{\rho}_{T}((y,h),(y^{\prime},h^{\prime}))^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T\hat{\sigma}_{T,\max}^{2}}\int_{0}^{T}\left(K_{y,h}(X_{s})-K_{y^{\prime},h^{\prime}}(X_{s})\right)^{2}ds

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3L^{*}\|K\|_{TV}}{\hat{\sigma}_{T,\max}^{2}}\frac{2(u\delta)^{2}\hat{\sigma}_{T,\max}^{2}}{6L^{*}\|K\|_{TV}}=(u\delta)^{2}

and the bound of the covering numbers follows with
B=4
and
V=2
.

To conclude asymptotic tightness, it suffices by Theorem
E.4
to verify

\inf_{T\geq T_{0}}\sup_{(y,h)\in\overline{\mathcal{T}}}\overline{\sigma}_{T}(y,h)>0

on
\mathcal{C}_{T,b_{0}}
. But this follows directly by the occupations times formula, as (
E.12
) holds on
\mathcal{C}_{T,b_{0}}
, and hence

\displaystyle\overline{\sigma}_{T}(y,h)^{2}=\frac{\int_{0}^{T}K_{y,h}(X_{s})^{2}ds}{\int_{0}^{T}\mathbbm{1}_{[-A,A]}(X_{s})ds}=\frac{\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{y,h}(z)\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}T}L_{T}^{z}(X)dz}{\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbbm{1}_{[-A,A]}(z)\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}T}L_{T}^{z}(X)dz}\geq\frac{\|K_{y,h}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}L_{*}}{6AL^{*}}.

The assertion of Theorem
3.1
now follows from the observation that
D(\cdot)
is bounded and stricly positive on
(0,1]
with
\lim_{r\searrow 0}D(r)=0
.
∎

The following result proves finiteness of the random variable
S_{b}
given in Theorem
7.1
and can be proven along the lines of the proof of Theorem
3.1
. This is fully worked out as Theorem
3.3.8
in
[
9
]
.

Theorem E.6
.

Let
K
be a compactly supported kernel function of bounded variation with
\|K\|_{\infty}\leq 1
. For
b\in\Sigma(C,A,\gamma,\sigma)
define

\sigma_{b}(y,h)^{2}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{y,h}(z)^{2}q_{b}(z)dz

and

\sigma_{b,\max}^{2}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbbm{1}_{[-A,A]}(z)q_{b}(z)dz.

Then for
\mathcal{T}
given in
(
3.3
)
,
\mathbb{P}_{b}
-almost surely,

\sup_{(y,h)\in\mathcal{T}}\left(\frac{\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{y,h}(z)\sqrt{q_{b}(z)}dW_{z}\right|}{\|K_{y,h}\sqrt{q_{b}}\|_{L^{2}}}-\Upsilon\left(\sigma_{b}(y,h)^{2}/\sigma_{b,\max}^{2}\right)\right)<\infty.

F
Proof of Theorem
7.1
and uniform weak convergence

This section presents the proof of the uniform weak convergence result in Theorem
7.1
. In Subsection
F.1
we introduce the notion of uniform weak convergence using the dual bounded Lipschitz metric. Furthermore, it comprises many auxiliar results on uniform weak convergence that are of interest on their own, in particular the important Proposition
7.3
. The proof of Theorem
7.1
, which is a consequence of Lemma
F.7
and this Proposition
7.3
, is then given in Subsection
F.2
.

F.1
Preliminaries on uniform weak convergence

In this part we deal with general results about uniform weak convergence. Here and subsequently, let
(E,d)
denote some separable metric space and
\mathcal{S}
a countable parameter space. To define weak convergence that is uniform over some class of parameters, we use the fact that weak convergence for probability measures (and for random variables via their corresponding measure) is metrized by the dual bounded Lipschitz metric. To introduce this metric, we define the bounded Lipschitz norm for a bounded Lipschitz function
f:E\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
as

\|f\|_{BL}:=\left(\|f\|_{\infty}+\sup_{x\neq y}\frac{|f(x)-f(y)|}{d(x,y)}\right)

and set

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{BL}(E):=\{f:E\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\mid\|f\|_{BL}\leq 1\}.

(F.1)

A sequence of
E
-valued random variables
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges weakly to
X
if and only if

d_{BL}(X_{n},X):=\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{BL}(E)}\left|\mathbb{E}[f(X_{n})]-\mathbb{E}[f(X)]\right|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}0,

see for example
[
54
]
, p. 73. This can be used to define uniform weak convergence in the following way.

Definition F.1
.

Let
\Theta
be a set of parameters and
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
a sequence of
E
-valued random variables, where
(E,d)
is a separable metric space. We say that
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges uniformly (over
\Theta
) in distribution to
X
if and only if

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}d_{BL}^{\theta}(X_{n},X):=\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{BL}(E)}\left|\mathbb{E}_{\theta}[f(X_{n})]-\mathbb{E}_{\theta}[f(X)]\right|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}0.

Lemma F.2
.

Let
(A_{n}(s))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(B_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be two real-valued stochastic processes, where for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
B_{n}\neq 0
and
A_{n}(s)
depends on some parameter
s\in\mathcal{S}
. Suppose that for all
\epsilon>0
,

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left|A_{n}(s)-A(s)\right|>\epsilon\right)\rightarrow 0

and

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(\left|B_{n}-B\right|>\epsilon\right)\rightarrow 0,

where
A(s)
and
B
are real-valued random variables satisfying
1\geq B\geq\beta>0
and
\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}|A(s)|\leq 1
. Then we have for all
\epsilon>0
,

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left|\frac{A_{n}(s)}{B_{n}}-\frac{A(s)}{B}\right|>\epsilon\right)\rightarrow 0.

This follows easily by using

\left|\frac{A_{n}(s)}{B_{n}}-\frac{A(s)}{B}\right|\leq\left|\frac{A_{n}(s)-A(s)}{B_{n}}\right|+\left|\frac{A(s)}{BB_{n}}\right|\left|B-B_{n}\right|.

∎

Next, we establish that a uniform continuous mapping theorem holds true for Lipschitz functions. This result is stated in
[
29
]
as Theorem
1
for the real-valued case, but the result can be likewise shown for arbitrary metric spaces and a proof is therefore omitted.

Lemma F.3
.

Let
(E,d)
be a separable metric space. Suppose that the
E
-valued sequence
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges uniformly (over
\Theta
) in distribution to
X
. If
h:E\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is Lipschitz continuous, then
h(X_{n})
converges uniformly (over
\Theta
) in distribution to
h(X)
.

Lemma F.4
.

Let
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued random variables whose distribution depends on a parameter
\theta\in\Theta
and the uniform convergence

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{BL}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left[|f(X_{n})-f(X)|\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}0

holds true. Furthermore, assume the family
(\mathbb{P}_{\theta}^{X})_{\theta\in\Theta}
to be tight. Then for every
\epsilon>0
there exists a compact set
K_{\epsilon}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for every
n\geq n_{0}
,

\inf_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(X_{n}\in K_{\epsilon}\right)\geq 1-\epsilon.

Let
\epsilon>0
. By assumption there exists a compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

\inf_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}(X\in K)\geq 1-\epsilon.

We assume
K
to be a closed ball of radius
r
centered at the origin, i.e.
K=\overline{B_{r}(0)}
. This can always be done, as any compact set in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is subset of such a ball by the theorem of Heine–Borel. Now pick
\delta>0
and define
K^{\delta}:=\overline{B_{r+\delta}(0)}
, together with

f_{K,\delta}(x):=\begin{cases}1&\textrm{ if }x\in K,\\
1-\frac{d(x,K)}{\delta}&\textrm{ if }x\in K^{\delta}\setminus K,\\
0&\textrm{ else},\end{cases}

where
d(x,K):=\inf_{z\in K}\|x-z\|_{2}
. The function
f_{K,\delta}
is Lipschitz continuous and we have
\mathbbm{1}_{K}\leq f_{K,\delta}\leq\mathbbm{1}_{K^{\delta}}
. This gives

\displaystyle\inf_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(X_{n}\in K^{\delta}\right)=\inf_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{K^{\delta}}(X_{n})\right]\geq\inf_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left[f_{K,\delta}(X_{n})\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}\inf_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left[f_{K,\delta}(X)\right]\geq\inf_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{K}(X)\right]=\inf_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}(X\in K).

The last term is greater than
1-\epsilon
and we find an integer
n_{0}
such that for
n\geq n_{0}
, we have
\inf_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(X_{n}\in K^{\delta}\right)\geq 1-2\epsilon
.
∎

The following result is a modification of Theorem
1.12.1
in
[
54
]
for uniform weak convergence and its proof follows the same ideas. In our later application of this result, the bounded and equicontinuous class
\mathcal{F}
used within this result will be chosen as
\mathcal{F}_{BL}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Lemma F.5
.

Let
\Theta
be a parameter space and
X,X_{1},X_{2}\dots
be
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued random variables such that the family
(\mathbb{P}_{\theta}^{X})_{\theta\in\Theta}
is tight, i.e. for every
\epsilon>0
there exists a compact set
K=K_{\epsilon}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

\inf_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}(X\in K)\geq 1-\epsilon.

Furthermore, we have for any bounded and continuous
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\left|\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left[f(X_{n})\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left[f(X)\right]\right|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}0.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a bounded and equicontinuous class of functions. Then the last convergence even holds true uniformly in
\mathcal{F}
, i.e.

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\left|\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left[f(X_{n})\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left[f(X)\right]\right|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}0.

Let
\epsilon>0
. Then choose
K^{\prime}
to suffice
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}(X\in K^{\prime})\geq 1-\epsilon
according to the assumption. Using Lemma
F.4
, there exists a compact set
K
with
K^{\prime}\subset K\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for
n\geq n_{0}
we have

\inf_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}(X_{n}\in K)\geq 1-\epsilon.

Denote by
\mathcal{F}_{K}
the set of all functions of
\mathcal{F}
restricted to the compact set
K
. Then by the Arzelà–Ascoli theorem,
\mathcal{F}_{K}
is totally bounded in
\mathcal{C}_{b}(K)
and there exist finitely many balls of radius
\epsilon
that cover
\mathcal{F}_{K}
. Denote their centers by
f_{1},\dots,f_{N}
. By Tietze’s theorem, we can extend them to elements of
\mathcal{C}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
which are again denoted by
f_{1},\dots,f_{N}
. Then, by assumption,

\displaystyle\max_{i=1,\dots,N}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\left|\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left[f(X_{n})\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left[f(X)\right]\right|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}0.

(F.2)

We split

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left[f(X_{n})\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left[f(X)\right]&=\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left[f(X_{n})\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left[f_{i}(X_{n})\right]\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt+\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left[f_{i}(X_{n})\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left[f_{i}(X)\right]\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt+\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left[f_{i}(X)\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left[f(X)\right].\end{split}

(F.3)

The first difference can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left[f(X_{n})\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left[f_{i}(X_{n})\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left[(f(X_{n})-f_{i}(X_{n}))\mathbbm{1}_{K}\right]+\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left[(f(X_{n})-f_{i}(X_{n}))\mathbbm{1}_{K^{c}}\right].

For the right choice of
i
the first term is bounded by
\epsilon
, as we can choose
f_{i}
to be the center of the
\epsilon
-ball (in supremum norm on
K
) that contains
f
. For the latter summand we have

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left[(f(X_{n})-f_{i}(X_{n}))\mathbbm{1}_{K^{c}}\right]\leq 2C^{\prime}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(X_{n}\in K^{c}\right)\leq 2C^{\prime}\epsilon,

for
n\geq n_{0}
and
C^{\prime}
being the uniform bound on
\mathcal{F}
. Additionally, we used

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(X_{n}\in K^{c}\right)=\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\left(1-\mathbb{P}(X_{n}\in K)\right)=1-\inf_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}(X_{n}\in K)\leq\epsilon.

The third difference in (
F.3
) can be treated in the same way and the second one is bounded by
\epsilon
for sufficiently large
n
by (
F.2
). Thus,

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\left|\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left[f(X_{n})\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left[f(X)\right]\right|\leq 3\epsilon+4C^{\prime}\epsilon,

and letting
\epsilon\searrow 0
concludes the proof.
∎

The following result can be proven by standard arguments.

Lemma F.6
.

Let
(E,d)
be a separable metric space and
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(Y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be two sequences of
E
-valued random variables such that for all
\epsilon>0
,

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(d(X_{n},Y_{n})>\epsilon\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}\ 0

and
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}d_{BL}^{\theta}(X_{n},X)\rightarrow 0
for some random variable
X
. Then,

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}d_{BL}^{\theta}(Y_{n},X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}\ 0.

Lemma F.7
.

Let sequences
(X_{n}(s))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
,
(\alpha_{n}(s))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\beta_{n}(s))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of real-valued stochastic processes together with real-valued random variables
\alpha(s),\beta(s),X(s)
depending on a parameter
s\in\mathcal{S}
be given. Assume the following conditions to be true for a parameter space
\Theta
:

(a)

We have the uniform weak convergence

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}d_{BL}^{\theta}\left(\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left(\frac{X_{n}(s)}{\alpha(s)}-\beta(s)\right),\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left(\frac{X(s)}{\alpha(s)}-\beta(s)\right)\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}\ 0.

(b)

We have
\alpha(s)\in(0,\infty)
and for every
\epsilon>0
there exist compact sets
K(\epsilon),K^{\prime}(\epsilon)\subset\mathbb{R}
with

\inf_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\beta(s)\in K(\epsilon)\right)\geq 1-\epsilon

and

\inf_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left(\frac{X(s)}{\alpha(s)}-\beta(s)\right)\in K^{\prime}(\epsilon)\right)\geq 1-\epsilon.

(c)

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\alpha_{n}(s)\in(0,\infty)
and for every
\epsilon>0
,

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left|\frac{\alpha(s)}{\alpha_{n}(s)}-1\right|>\epsilon\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}0.

(d)

For every
\epsilon>0
,

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}|\beta_{n}(s)-\beta(s)|>\epsilon\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}0.

Then we have

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}d_{BL}^{\theta}\left(\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left(\frac{X_{n}(s)}{\alpha_{n}(s)}-\beta_{n}(s)\right),\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left(\frac{X(s)}{\alpha(s)}-\beta(s)\right)\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}\ 0.

We want to apply Lemma
F.6
and therefore need to show in addition to assumption (a) that for all
\epsilon>0
,

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(\left|\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left(\frac{X_{n}(s)}{\alpha_{n}(s)}-\beta_{n}(s)\right)-\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left(\frac{X_{n}(s)}{\alpha(s)}-\beta(s)\right)\right|>\epsilon\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}0.

By upper bounding the random variable within the probability, we find

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(\left|\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left(\frac{X_{n}(s)}{\alpha_{n}(s)}-\beta_{n}(s)\right)-\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left(\frac{X_{n}(s)}{\alpha(s)}-\beta(s)\right)\right|>\epsilon\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 2.84544pt\leq\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left|\left(\frac{X_{n}(s)}{\alpha_{n}(s)}-\beta_{n}(s)-\frac{X_{n}(s)}{\alpha(s)}+\beta(s)\right)\right|>\epsilon\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 2.84544pt=\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left|\left(\frac{X_{n}(s)}{\alpha(s)}-\beta(s)\right)\left(\frac{\alpha(s)}{\alpha_{n}(s)}-1\right)+\frac{\alpha(s)}{\alpha_{n}(s)}\beta(s)-\beta_{n}(s)\right|>\epsilon\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 2.84544pt\leq\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left|\frac{X_{n}(s)}{\alpha(s)}-\beta(s)\right|\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left|\frac{\alpha(s)}{\alpha_{n}(s)}-1\right|\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt\left.+\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left|\frac{\alpha(s)}{\alpha_{n}(s)}\beta(s)-\beta_{n}(s)\right|>\epsilon\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 2.84544pt\leq\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left|\frac{X_{n}(s)}{\alpha(s)}-\beta(s)\right|\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left|\frac{\alpha(s)}{\alpha_{n}(s)}-1\right|>\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt+\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left|\frac{\alpha(s)}{\alpha_{n}(s)}\beta(s)-\beta_{n}(s)\right|>\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right).

Those last two summands will be treated separately. Let
\delta>0
and choose a corresponding
K=K(\delta)>0
such that

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left|\frac{X(s)}{\alpha(s)}-\beta(s)\right|>K\right)<\delta,

which is possible by assumption (b). Using
\sup_{\theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}(A)=1-\inf_{\theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}(A^{c})

and Lemma
F.4
, for some
n\geq n_{0}
and
K^{\prime}\geq K>0
,

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left|\frac{X_{n}(s)}{\alpha(s)}-\beta(s)\right|>K^{\prime}\right)<\delta.

Proceeding with this,

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left|\frac{X_{n}(s)}{\alpha(s)}-\beta(s)\right|\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left|\frac{\alpha(s)}{\alpha_{n}(s)}-1\right|>\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69046pt\leq\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(K^{\prime}\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left|\frac{\alpha(s)}{\alpha_{n}(s)}-1\right|>\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right)+\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left|\frac{X_{n}(s)}{\alpha(s)}-\beta(s)\right|>K^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69046pt\leq\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left|\frac{\alpha(s)}{\alpha_{n}(s)}-1\right|>\frac{\epsilon}{2K^{\prime}}\right)+\delta

and the probability converges to zero for
n\to\infty
by condition (c). For the other summand at the end of our initial estimate, we proceed in a similar manner. We choose
L
large enough such that

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}|\beta(s)|>L\right)<\delta,

which is possible with regard to our assumption (b). Then,

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left|\frac{\alpha(s)}{\alpha_{n}(s)}\beta(s)-\beta_{n}(s)\right|>\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left|\left(\frac{\alpha(s)}{\alpha_{n}(s)}-1\right)\beta(s)+\beta(s)-\beta_{n}(s)\right|>\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left|\left(\frac{\alpha(s)}{\alpha_{n}(s)}-1\right)\beta(s)\right|+\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}|\beta(s)-\beta_{n}(s)|>\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left|\left(\frac{\alpha(s)}{\alpha_{n}(s)}-1\right)\beta(s)\right|>\frac{\epsilon}{4}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt+\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}|\beta(s)-\beta_{n}(s)|>\frac{\epsilon}{4}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left|\frac{\alpha(s)}{\alpha_{n}(s)}-1\right|>\frac{\epsilon}{4L}\right)+\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}|\beta(s)|>L\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt+\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}|\beta(s)-\beta_{n}(s)|>\frac{\epsilon}{4}\right).

The middle summand here is bounded by
\delta
and the others both converge to zero by condition (c) and (d). The assertion of the lemma follows by letting
\delta\searrow 0
.
∎

For a pseudometric space
(\mathcal{S},\rho)
and
u>0
, the
covering numbers

N(u,\mathcal{S},\rho)
are defined by

\displaystyle N(u,\mathcal{S},\rho):=\min\left\{\#\mathcal{S}_{0}:\mathcal{S}_{0}\subset\mathcal{S}\textrm{ and }\inf_{s_{0}\in\mathcal{S}_{0}}\rho(s,s_{0})\leq u\textrm{ for all }s\in\mathcal{S}\right\},

(F.4)

Subsequently, a proof of Proposition
7.3
is given. This result is an extension of Theorem
8
in
[
42
]
to uniform weak convergence.

For every natural number
k\in\mathbb{N}
let
\mathcal{S}_{\theta}^{k}
be some maximal subset of
\mathcal{S}
such that
\rho_{\theta}(s,s^{\prime})\geq\frac{1}{k}
for any
t,t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{\theta}^{k}
, and
\mathcal{S}_{\theta}^{1}\subset\mathcal{S}_{\theta}^{2}\subset\cdots
. Now define

\lambda_{\theta}^{k}(s,u):=\frac{\left(1-k\rho_{\theta}(s,u)\right)_{+}}{\sum_{v\in\mathcal{S}_{\theta}^{k}}\left(1-k\rho_{\theta}(s,v)\right)_{+}}

for all
s\in\mathcal{S}
and
u\in\mathcal{S}_{\theta}^{k}
. It satisfies

\lambda_{\theta}^{k}(s,u)=0\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{ if }\hskip 10.00002pt\rho_{\theta}(s,u))\geq\frac{1}{k}

and

\sum_{u\in\mathcal{S}_{\theta}^{k}}\lambda_{\theta}^{k}(\cdot,u)=1.

Moreover,
0\leq\lambda_{\theta}^{k}(\cdot,u)\in\mathcal{C}_{u}(\mathcal{S},\rho_{\theta})
, where

\mathcal{C}_{u}(\mathcal{S},\rho_{\theta})=\left\{f\in l_{\infty}(\mathcal{S}):\ \lim_{\delta\searrow 0}\sup_{s,t\in\mathcal{S}:\rho_{\theta}(s,t)<\delta}|f(t)-f(s)|=0\right\}.

Define
\pi_{\theta}^{k}:l_{\infty}(\mathcal{S})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{u}(\mathcal{S},\rho_{\theta})
by

\pi_{\theta}^{k}f:=\sum_{u\in\mathcal{S}_{\theta}^{k}}f(u)\lambda_{\theta}^{k}(\cdot,u).

Then for all
f\in l_{\infty}(\mathcal{S})
,

\displaystyle\|\pi_{\theta}^{k}f\|_{\mathcal{S}}\leq\|f\|_{\mathcal{S}_{\theta}^{k}}\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt\|f-\pi_{\theta}^{k}f\|_{\mathcal{S}}\leq\sup_{\rho_{\theta}(s,s^{\prime})\leq\frac{1}{k}}|f(s)-f(s^{\prime})|,

(F.5)

and
\pi_{\theta}^{k}
is a linear map with Lipschitz constant one because for
f,g\in l_{\infty}(\mathcal{S})
,

\displaystyle\left|(\pi_{\theta}^{k}f)(s)-(\pi_{\theta}^{k}g)(s)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{u\in\mathcal{S}_{\theta}^{k}}(f(u)-g(u))\lambda_{\theta}^{k}(s,u)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{u\in\mathcal{S}_{\theta}^{k}}\left|f(u)-g(u)\right|\sum_{u\in\mathcal{S}_{\theta}^{k}}\lambda_{\theta}^{k}(s,u)

\displaystyle\leq\|f-g\|_{\mathcal{S}}.

In particular, for any
f:l_{\infty}(\mathcal{S})\rightarrow[0,1]
with Lipschitz constant
L
, the composition
f\circ\pi_{\theta}^{k}
again takes values in
[0,1]
and has Lipschitz constant
L
. Now we split

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{BL}(l_{\infty}(\mathcal{S}))}\left|\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left[f(X_{n})\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left[f(Y_{n})\right]\right|

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\leq\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{BL}(l_{\infty}(\mathcal{S}))}\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left[\left|f(X_{n})-f(\pi_{\theta}^{k}X_{n})\right|\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{BL}(l_{\infty}(\mathcal{S}))}\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left[\left|f(\pi_{\theta}^{k}X_{n})-f(\pi_{\theta}^{k}Y_{n})\right|\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{BL}(l_{\infty}(\mathcal{S}))}\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left[\left|f(\pi_{\theta}^{k}Y_{n})-f(Y_{n})\right|\right].

We have
\{f\circ\pi_{\theta}^{k}\mid f\in\mathcal{F}_{BL}(l_{\infty}(\mathcal{S}))\}\subset\mathcal{F}_{BL}(l_{\infty}(\mathcal{S}_{\theta}^{k}))
and assumption (c) on the covering numbers yields
\sup_{\theta}|\mathcal{S}_{\theta}^{k}|<\infty
. By assumption (a),

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{BL}(l_{\infty}(\mathcal{S}))}\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left[\left|f(\pi_{\theta}^{k}X_{n})-f(\pi_{\theta}^{k}Y_{n})\right|\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}\ 0.

Let
\epsilon>0
. By assumption (b) there exists a natural number
l=l(\epsilon)
such that for
Z_{n}=X_{n},Y_{n}
we have

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(\sup_{\rho_{\theta}(s,s^{\prime})\leq\frac{1}{l}}|Z_{n}(s)-Z_{n}(s^{\prime})|>\epsilon\right)<\epsilon.

By the bounded Lipschitz property of
f
and (
F.5
) we get for this number
l
,

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{BL}(l_{\infty}(\mathcal{S}))}\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left[\left|f(Z_{n})-f(\pi_{\theta}^{l}Z_{n})\right|\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{BL}(l_{\infty}(\mathcal{S}))}\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left[\left|f(Z_{n})-f(\pi_{\theta}^{l}Z_{n})\right|\mathbbm{1}_{\{\|Z_{n}-\pi_{\theta}^{l}Z_{n}\|_{\mathcal{S}}\leq\epsilon\}}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{BL}(l_{\infty}(\mathcal{S}))}\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left[\left|f(Z_{n})-f(\pi_{\theta}^{l}Z_{n})\right|\mathbbm{1}_{\{\|Z_{n}-\pi_{\theta}^{l}Z_{n}\|_{\mathcal{S}}>\epsilon\}}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\leq\epsilon+2\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(\|Z_{n}-\pi_{\theta}^{l}Z_{n}\|_{\mathcal{S}}>\epsilon\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\leq\epsilon+2\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(\sup_{\rho_{\theta}(s,s^{\prime})\leq\frac{1}{l}}|Z_{n}(s)-Z_{n}(s^{\prime})|>\epsilon\right)<3\epsilon.

Letting
\epsilon\searrow 0
completes the proof.
∎

F.2
Proof of Theorem
7.1

Throughout the proof we will abbreviate
\Sigma:=\Sigma(C,A,\gamma,\sigma)
. Moreover, we define

Z_{T}(y,h):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\int_{0}^{T}K_{y,h}(X_{s})dW_{s}

and

Z_{b}(y,h):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{y,h}(z)\sqrt{q_{b}(z)}dW_{z}.

We remember the notation

\hat{\sigma}_{T}(y,h)^{2}=\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}K_{y,h}(X_{s})^{2}ds\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt\hat{\sigma}_{T,\max}^{2}=\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbbm{1}_{[-A,A]}(X_{s})ds

from Section
E
, together with
\overline{\sigma}_{T}(y,h)=\frac{\hat{\sigma}_{T}(y,h)}{\hat{\sigma}_{T,\max}}
and the semimetric

\overline{\rho}_{T}((y,h),(y^{\prime},h^{\prime}))^{2}=\frac{1}{T\hat{\sigma}_{T,\max}^{2}}\int_{0}^{T}\left(K_{y,h}(X_{s})-K_{y^{\prime},h^{\prime}}(X_{s})\right)^{2}ds.

We define the deterministic counterparts

\displaystyle\sigma_{b}(y,h)^{2}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{y,h}(z)^{2}q_{b}(z)dz\ \ \textrm{ and }\ \ \sigma_{b,\max}^{2}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbbm{1}_{[-A,A]}(z)q_{b}(z)dz

(F.6)

on the set
\mathcal{T}
given in (
3.3
), and the corresponding semimetric on
\mathcal{T}\times\mathcal{T}
by

\rho_{b}((y,h),(y^{\prime},h^{\prime}))^{2}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(K_{y,h}(z)-K_{y^{\prime},h^{\prime}}(z)\right)^{2}q_{b}(z)dz.

Last but not least, we set

\displaystyle\overline{\sigma}_{b}(y,h):=\frac{\sigma_{b}(y,h)}{\sigma_{b,\max}}\ \ \textrm{ and }\ \ \overline{\rho}_{b}((y,h),(y^{\prime},h^{\prime})):=\frac{\rho_{b}((y,h),(y^{\prime},h^{\prime}))}{\sigma_{b,\max}}.

(F.7)

By an application of Proposition
D.4
one directly gets the following result about these quantities.

Lemma F.8
.

For the norms and seminorms defined above, we have for every
\epsilon>0
that

\lim_{T\to\infty}\sup_{b\in\Sigma}\mathbb{P}_{b}\left(\|\overline{\sigma}_{T}-\overline{\sigma}_{b}\|_{\mathcal{T}}>\epsilon\right)=0

and

\lim_{T\to\infty}\sup_{b\in\Sigma}\mathbb{P}_{b}\left(\|\overline{\rho}_{T}-\overline{\rho}_{b}\|_{\mathcal{T}\times\mathcal{T}}>\epsilon\right)=0.

The proof follows the steps (i)–(iii) given in the sketch of proof in Subsection
7.2
which are given here again with some additional details:

(i)

First, we establish for
0<c\leq A
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{c}=\{(y,h)\in\mathcal{T}\mid h\geq c\}

(F.8)

the uniform weak convergence of
(Z_{T}(y,h))_{(y,h)\in\mathcal{T}_{c}}
to
(Z_{b}(y,h))_{(y,h)\in\mathcal{T}_{c}}
with Proposition
7.3
.

(ii)

Secondly, we define

\displaystyle T_{T}(\delta,\delta^{\prime}):=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}(y,h)\in\mathcal{T}:\\
\delta<\overline{\sigma}_{b}(y,h)\leq\delta^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{\left|Z_{T}(y,h)\right|}{\hat{\sigma}_{T}(y,h)}-\Upsilon\bigg(\frac{\hat{\sigma}_{T}(y,h)^{2}}{\hat{\sigma}_{T,\max}^{2}}\bigg)\right)

(F.9)

as well as

\displaystyle S_{b}(\delta,\delta^{\prime}):=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}(y,h)\in\mathcal{T}:\\
\delta<\overline{\sigma}_{b}(y,h)\leq\delta^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{\left|Z_{b}(y,h)\right|}{\sigma_{b}(y,h)}-\Upsilon\bigg(\frac{\sigma_{b}(y,h)^{2}}{\sigma_{b,\max}^{2}}\bigg)\right)

and prove uniform weak convergence of
T_{T}(\delta,1)
to
S_{b}(\delta,1)
for every
c^{\prime}>\delta>0
and some constant
c^{\prime}>0
. This is based on the uniform continuous mapping result in Lemma
F.3
and Lemma
F.7
.

(iii)

Finally, we improve the result from (ii) to establish the desired weak convergence of
T_{T}(0,1)
to
S_{b}(0,1)
.

In the following, we will work through step (i), (ii) and (iii).

Step (i).
This result is established by using Proposition
7.3
. To prove prerequisite (a) of it, we first pick
(y_{1},h_{1}),\dots,(y_{N},h_{N})\in\overline{\mathcal{T}}_{c}
where
\overline{\mathcal{T}}_{c}
is given by

\overline{\mathcal{T}}_{c}:=\left\{(y,h)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\mid c\leq h\leq A\textrm{ and }-A+h\leq y\leq A-h\right\}.

For all
\epsilon>0
and
1\leq i,j\leq N
we have by Proposition
D.4
,

\displaystyle\lim_{T\to\infty}\sup_{b\in\Sigma}\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}K_{y_{i},h_{i}}(X_{s})K_{y_{j},h_{j}}(X_{s})ds\right.\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt\left.\left.-\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{y_{i},h_{i}}(z)K_{y_{j},h_{j}}(z)q_{b}(z)dz\right|>\epsilon\right)=0.

In consequence, by Proposition
1.21
in
[
34
]
,

\lim_{T\to\infty}\sup_{b\in\Sigma}\left|\mathbb{E}_{b}\left[f((Z_{T}(y_{k},h_{k}))_{1\leq k\leq N})\right]-\mathbb{E}_{b}\left[f((Z_{b}(y_{k},h_{k}))_{1\leq k\leq N})\right]\right|=0

for any bounded and continuous function
f:\mathbb{R}^{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
.
In order to apply Lemma
F.5
and conclude

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\lim_{T\to\infty}\sup_{b\in\Sigma}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{BL}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\left|\mathbb{E}_{b}\left[f((Z_{T}(y_{k},h_{k}))_{1\leq k\leq N})\right]\right.\\
&\hskip 113.81102pt\left.-\mathbb{E}_{b}\left[f((Z_{b}(y_{k},h_{k}))_{1\leq k\leq N})\right]\right|=0,\end{split}

(F.10)

we have to show uniform tightness of
(Z_{b}(y_{k},h_{k}))_{1\leq k\leq N}
. Using Markov’s inequality and Itô’s isometry, we find

\displaystyle\sup_{b\in\Sigma}\mathbb{P}_{b}\left(\|Z_{b}(y_{k},h_{k})\|_{2}^{2}\geq r\right)

\displaystyle=\sup_{b\in\Sigma}\mathbb{P}_{b}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{N}Z_{b}(y_{k},h_{k})^{2}\geq r\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{r}\sup_{b\in\Sigma}\mathbb{E}_{b}\left[\sum_{k=1}^{N}Z_{b}(y_{k},h_{k})^{2}\right]
