Inserting all these estimates into (
G.14
) yields

V_{-A}^{A}\left(f_{y,h}^{1}\cdot f_{y,h}^{2}\right)\leq h^{1/4}\left(2\sigma^{-2}\|K\|_{L^{2}}^{-1}C(1+A)\sqrt{L^{*}/L_{*}}+c\|K\|_{L^{2}}^{-1}V_{-1}^{1}(K)\right).

Finally, bounding
h\leq A
gives a finite bound independent of
y
and
h
.
∎

H
Proof of the minimax results

In Subsection
H.1
, a proof of Theorem
5.1
is given and in Subsection
H.2
a sketch of proof for the upper bound in Theorem
5.2
is provided.

H.1
Proof of Theorem
5.1

In this section, a proof of the minimax lower bound given in Theorem
5.1
is provided. This follows the sketch of proof given in Section
8
.

Like in most other proofs of lower bounds, we will reduce the problem to considering suitable challenging hypotheses – in our case some small deviations of the drift
b_{0}\pm\eta
– that will not be detected by an arbitrary test. Their existence then implies that we cannot detect smaller deviations, giving the lower bound for the hypothesis testing problem. In order to construct those, we set

\displaystyle h_{T}^{w}:=\left(\frac{c_{*}}{L}\right)^{\frac{1}{\beta}}\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}\log T}{Tw}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\beta+1}}

(H.1)

for a parameter
w\in\mathbb{R}
to be specified later. Assume we want to test for the
\eta
-environment of
b_{0}
. Then we pick some drift
b_{0}\in\Sigma\left(\frac{C}{2}-\eta,A,\gamma+\frac{\eta}{\sigma^{2}},\sigma\right)
for the zero hypothesis, consider

\displaystyle b_{0,\eta}:=b_{0}+\eta\in H_{0}(b_{0},\eta)

(H.2)

as a drift function on the boundary of our hypothesis and define an alternative by adding a small hat as

\displaystyle b^{w}(x):=b_{0,\eta}(x)+L(1-\epsilon_{T})(h_{T}^{w})^{\beta}K_{T}^{\beta}\left(\frac{x-y^{w}}{h_{T}^{w}}\right),

(H.3)

where

y:[L_{*},L^{*}]\rightarrow\left[-A+h_{T}^{L_{*}},A-h_{T}^{L_{*}}\right]=:[-A^{\prime},A^{\prime}]

is a function depending continuously on
w
that describes the location our added hat is put on.
K_{T}^{\beta}
is given as a truncated version of the optimal recovery kernel
K_{\beta}
, i.e.

\displaystyle K_{T}^{\beta}(x):=\begin{cases}K_{\beta}(x)&\textrm{ if }\beta\geq 1,\\
K_{\beta}(x)\mathbbm{1}_{\{\frac{1}{T}\leq|x|\leq 1\}}+(1-\frac{1}{T^{\beta}})\mathbbm{1}_{\{|x|\leq\frac{1}{T}\}}&\textrm{ if }\beta<1.\end{cases}

To be a valid hypothesis we need three conditions on
b^{w}
:

•

The solution to SDE (
2.1
) with drift
b^{w}
should be ergodic, more precisely, we need
b^{w}\in\Sigma(C,A,\gamma,\sigma)
,

•

we need
b^{w}-b_{0}\in\mathcal{H}(\beta,L)
, and

•

we want
\Delta_{J}(b)\geq(1-\epsilon_{T})c_{*}\delta_{T}
. As the boundary case of equality is expected to be the most challenging, we require equality, i.e.

\displaystyle\left|(b^{w}(y^{w})-b_{0}(y^{w}))\left(\frac{q_{b^{w}}(y^{w})}{\sigma^{2}}\right)^{\frac{\beta}{2\beta+1}}\right|

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=L(1-\epsilon_{T})(h_{T}^{w})^{\beta}K_{T}^{\beta}(0)\left(\frac{q_{b^{w}}(y^{w})}{\sigma^{2}}\right)^{\frac{\beta}{2\beta+1}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=(1-\epsilon_{T})c_{*}\left(\frac{\log T}{T}\right)^{\frac{\beta}{2\beta+1}}.

Using our definition of
h_{T}^{w}
in (
H.1
), the last equality is equivalent to the fixed point equation

\displaystyle w=q_{b^{w}}(y^{w})K_{T}^{\beta}(0)^{\frac{2\beta+1}{\beta}}.

(H.4)

As we are dealing with asymptotics, it is in fact enough to verify those conditions for
T\geq T_{0}
. The first two are standard and will be established in Lemma
H.2
and
H.3
.
The first one is the reason why we modified the kernel
K_{\beta}
for
\beta<1
. In fact, we have to check that
b^{w}\in\textrm{Lip}_{\textrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R})
which is easily obtained for
\beta\geq 1
, but not true for
\beta<1
since the kernel is only Hölder continuous. As the proof of the lower bound will find the optimal constant, it is nonetheless necessary that
K_{T}^{\beta}
is chosen in such a way that its
L^{2}
-norm approximates the
L^{2}
-norm of
K_{\beta}
sufficiently fast. It is stated in Lemma
H.1
that
K_{T}^{\beta}
has all the desired properties.

The third condition will be dealt with in the proof of the main theorem by constructing the alternatives inductively. It has the nice feature that is gives rise to the fixed-point problem (
H.4
) : We associate to any
w
in some suitable chosen interval a drift function
b^{w}
which again is associated to a density function
q_{b^{w}}
which is known in the ergodic case. This density can be evaluated at
y^{w}
and we ask if it possible to chose
w
in such a way that the outcome of the concatenation of these mappings is again
w
times a constant. In Lemma
8.2
we prove this to be solvable.

Later in the proof of Theorem
5.1
, we have to use different hypotheses of this kind at different locations. The construction of those will be an iterative procedure of applying Lemma
8.2
for several locations leading to different
w_{i}
. But to keep notation simpler, we first show the important properties for a single
b^{w}
.

Lemma H.1
(
[
9
]
, Lemma 3.6.1).

Let
\beta<1
. Then
K_{T}^{\beta}\in\textrm{Lip}_{\textrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R})
and
K_{T}^{\beta}\in\mathcal{H}(\beta,1)
. Furthermore, we have

\left|\|K_{T}^{\beta}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}-\|K_{\beta}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right|\leq\sqrt{8}\|K_{\beta}\|_{L^{2}}T^{-\frac{1}{2}-\beta}.

Lemma H.2
(
[
9
]
, Lemma 3.6.2).

Let
b_{0}\in\Sigma(\frac{C}{2}-\eta,A,\gamma+\frac{\eta}{\sigma^{2}},\sigma)
and
b^{w}
be given as in
(
H.3
)
. Then we have
b^{w}-b_{0}\in\mathcal{H}(\beta,L)
.

Lemma H.3
(
[
9
]
, Lemma 3.6.3).

For the drift
b^{w}
given in
(
H.3
)
, we have
b^{w}\in\Sigma(C,A,\gamma,\sigma)
for all
T
large enough to ensure
L(h_{T}^{w})^{\beta}\leq\frac{C}{2}
.

Next, we prove Lemma
8.2
that provides the solution to our fixed point problem.

Denote by
f
the concatenation of the following maps

\begin{matrix}&[c_{T}L_{*},c_{T}L^{*}]&\longrightarrow&\mathcal{C}([-A,A])\times[-A,A]&\longrightarrow&\mathbb{R},\\
&w&\mapsto&\begin{pmatrix}q_{b^{w}}\\
y^{w}\end{pmatrix}&\mapsto&c_{T}q_{b^{w}}(y^{w}),\end{matrix}

(H.5)

where
\mathcal{C}([-A,A])
is equipped with
\|\cdot\|_{[-A,A]}
. Our proof will establish

(1)

continuity of
f:[c_{T}L_{*},c_{T}L^{*}]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
,

(2)

existence of a compact interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that
f(I)\subset I
.

Then it follows that there exists a fixed point
\tilde{w}
within the interval
I
by the intermediate value theorem and the proof is complete.

Statement
(2)
is straightforward, as we know that each invariant density
q_{b^{w}}
only takes values in
[L_{*},L^{*}]
by Lemma
D.1
. With our specification of
c_{T}
this implies
c_{T}q_{b^{w}}\in[c_{T}L_{*},c_{T}L^{*}]
. It remains to deal with statement
(1)
. The map
f
consists of two parts and we will show continuity of each mapping in (
H.5
) separately, each time for fixed
T
.

We start with the first mapping in (
H.5
). Clearly, the map
w\mapsto h_{T}^{w}
is continuous and hence
w\mapsto y+Rh_{T}^{w}=y^{w}
is continuous, as well. For
w\mapsto q_{b}
we note that the invariant density is of the form

q_{b^{w}}(x)=\frac{1}{C_{b^{w},\sigma}}\exp\left(\int_{0}^{x}\frac{2b^{w}(u)}{\sigma^{2}}du\right),

and we show continuity of
w\mapsto C_{b^{w},\sigma}
and
w\mapsto\exp\left(\int_{0}^{x}\frac{2b^{w}(u)}{\sigma^{2}}du\right)
, where the right-hand side of the latter is understood as a function restricted to the interval
[-A,A]
. For the second statement, we consider
w,w^{\prime}>0
and estimate

\displaystyle\left|\exp\left(\int_{0}^{x}\frac{2b^{w}(u)}{\sigma^{2}}du\right)-\exp\left(\int_{0}^{x}\frac{2b^{w^{\prime}}(u)}{\sigma^{2}}du\right)\right|

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=\exp(\xi)\left|\int_{0}^{x}\frac{2b^{w}(u)}{\sigma^{2}}du-\int_{0}^{x}\frac{2b^{w^{\prime}}(u)}{\sigma^{2}}du\right|,

where
\xi
lies between
\int_{0}^{x}2\sigma^{-2}b^{w}(u)du
and
\int_{0}^{x}2\sigma^{-2}b^{w^{\prime}}(u)du
.
In particular, as

\left|\int_{0}^{x}\frac{2b^{w}(u)}{\sigma^{2}}du\right|\leq 2\sigma^{-2}\int_{0}^{A}C(1+u)du\leq 2C\sigma^{-2}(1+A)A

by the at most linear growth condition on
b
, we find

\displaystyle\left|\exp\left(\int_{0}^{x}\frac{2b^{w}(u)}{\sigma^{2}}du\right)-\exp\left(\int_{0}^{x}\frac{2b^{w^{\prime}}(u)}{\sigma^{2}}du\right)\right|

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\leq e^{2C\sigma^{-2}A(1+A)}\left|\int_{0}^{x}\frac{2b^{w}(u)}{\sigma^{2}}du-\int_{0}^{x}\frac{2b^{w^{\prime}}(u)}{\sigma^{2}}du\right|.

Writing
b^{w}=b_{0,\eta}+g^{w}
,

\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{x}\frac{2b^{w}(u)}{\sigma^{2}}du-\int_{0}^{x}\frac{2b^{w^{\prime}}(u)}{\sigma^{2}}du\right|

\displaystyle=2\sigma^{-2}\left|\int_{0}^{x}b^{w}(u)-b^{w^{\prime}}(u)du\right|

\displaystyle\leq 2\sigma^{-2}\textrm{sgn}(x)\int_{0}^{x}\left|g^{w}(u)-g^{w^{\prime}}(u)\right|du

\displaystyle\leq 2\sigma^{-2}\int_{-A}^{A}\left|g^{w}(u)-g^{w^{\prime}}(u)\right|du

\displaystyle\leq 4A\sigma^{-2}\|g^{w}-g^{w^{\prime}}\|_{[-A,A]}

\displaystyle=4A\sigma^{-2}\|b^{w}-b^{w^{\prime}}\|_{[-A,A]},

which gives the intermediate result

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sup_{x\in[-A,A]}\left|\exp\left(\int_{0}^{x}\frac{2b^{w}(u)}{\sigma^{2}}du\right)-\exp\left(\int_{0}^{x}\frac{2b^{w^{\prime}}(u)}{\sigma^{2}}du\right)\right|\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt\leq 4A\sigma^{-2}e^{2C\sigma^{-2}A(1+A)}\|b^{w}-b^{w^{\prime}}\|_{[-A,A]}.\end{split}

(H.6)

We move on to
w\mapsto C_{b^{w},\sigma}
and show that the difference of
C_{b^{w},\sigma}
and
C_{b^{w^{\prime}},\sigma}
can also be bounded by a multiple of
\|b^{w}-b^{w^{\prime}}\|_{[-A,A]}
. By the triangle inequality we directly get

\displaystyle\left|C_{b^{w},\sigma}-C_{b^{w^{\prime}},\sigma}\right|

\displaystyle\hskip 8.5359pt\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|\exp\left(\int_{0}^{x}2\sigma^{-2}b^{w}(u)du\right)-\exp\left(\int_{0}^{x}2\sigma^{-2}b^{w^{\prime}}(u)du\right)\right|dx

\displaystyle\hskip 8.5359pt=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\exp\left(\int_{0}^{x}2\sigma^{-2}b_{0,\eta}(u)du\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\cdot\left|\exp\left(\int_{0}^{x}2\sigma^{-2}g^{w}(u)du\right)-\exp\left(\int_{0}^{x}2\sigma^{-2}g^{w^{\prime}}(u)du\right)\right|dx

\displaystyle\hskip 8.5359pt\leq C_{b_{0,\eta},\sigma}\left\|\exp\left(\int_{0}^{\cdot}2\sigma^{-2}g^{w}(u)du\right)-\exp\left(\int_{0}^{\cdot}2\sigma^{-2}g^{w^{\prime}}(u)du\right)\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\hskip 8.5359pt=C_{b_{0,\eta},\sigma}\hskip-1.42271pt\left\|\exp\left(\int_{0}^{\cdot}2\sigma^{-2}g^{w}(u)du\right)\hskip-1.42271pt-\hskip-1.42271pt\exp\left(\int_{0}^{\cdot}2\sigma^{-2}g^{w^{\prime}}(u)du\right)\right\|_{[-A,A]},

where the last step follows from the fact that all
g^{w}
are supported within
[-A,A]
. Then we can proceed as above. In consequence, by (
H.6
),

\displaystyle\left|C_{b^{w},\sigma}-C_{b^{w^{\prime}},\sigma}\right|\leq 4A\sigma^{-2}C_{b_{0,\eta},\sigma}e^{2C\sigma^{-2}A(1+A)}\|b^{w}-b^{w^{\prime}}\|_{[-A,A]}.

(H.7)

We now turn to the evaluation of
\|b^{w}-b^{w^{\prime}}\|_{[-A,A]}
. First, we have pointwise

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left|b^{w}(x)-b^{w^{\prime}}(x)\right|\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt\leq L(1-\epsilon_{T})\left|(h_{T}^{w})^{\beta}K_{T}^{\beta}\left(\frac{x-y^{w}}{h_{T}^{w}}\right)-(h_{T}^{w^{\prime}})^{\beta}K_{T}^{\beta}\left(\frac{x-y^{w^{\prime}}}{h_{T}^{w^{\prime}}}\right)\right|\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt\leq L(1-\epsilon_{T})\left|\left((h_{T}^{w})^{\beta}-(h_{T}^{w^{\prime}})^{\beta}\right)K_{T}^{\beta}\left(\frac{x-y^{w}}{h_{T}^{w}}\right)\right|\\
&\hskip 71.13188pt+(h_{T}^{w^{\prime}})^{\beta}\left|K_{T}^{\beta}\left(\frac{x-y^{w}}{h_{T}^{w}}\right)-K_{T}^{\beta}\left(\frac{x-y^{w^{\prime}}}{h_{T}^{w^{\prime}}}\right)\right|.\end{split}

(H.8)

The first summand is clearly bounded by
L|(h_{T}^{w})^{\beta}-(h_{T}^{w^{\prime}})^{\beta}|
as we have
\|K_{T}^{\beta}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
and
1-\epsilon_{T}\leq 1
. For the second summand, we distinguish the cases
\beta\leq 1
and
\beta>1
. In the first one, the Hölder property gives the upper bound

(h_{T}^{w^{\prime}})^{\beta}\left|\frac{x-y^{w^{\prime}}}{h_{T}^{w^{\prime}}}-\frac{x-y^{w}}{h_{T}^{w}}\right|^{\beta}=(h_{T}^{w^{\prime}})^{\beta}\left|\frac{h_{T}^{w}(x-y^{w^{\prime}})-h_{T}^{w^{\prime}}(x-y^{w})}{h_{T}^{w^{\prime}}h_{T}^{w}}\right|^{\beta}.

For
\beta>1
we know that
K_{T}^{\beta}=K_{\beta}
is differentiable and by the Hölder condition on its derivative, this derivative is continuous. Since the support of
K_{\beta}
is bounded,
K_{\beta}^{\prime}
attains its maximum and
\|K_{\beta}^{\prime}\|_{\infty}<\infty
. In this case we have the bound

(h_{T}^{w^{\prime}})^{\beta}\|K_{\beta}^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\left|\frac{h_{T}^{w}(x-y^{w^{\prime}})-h_{T}^{w^{\prime}}(x-y^{w})}{h_{T}^{w^{\prime}}h_{T}^{w}}\right|.

Using the definition of
y^{w}
we find

\displaystyle h_{T}^{w}(x-y^{w^{\prime}})-h_{T}^{w^{\prime}}(x-y^{w})

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=(h_{T}^{w}-h_{T}^{w^{\prime}})x-h_{T}^{w}(y^{w^{\prime}}-y^{w})+y^{w}(h_{T}^{w^{\prime}}-h_{T}^{w})

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=(h_{T}^{w}-h_{T}^{w^{\prime}})\left(x+Rh_{T}^{w}-y^{w}\right),

which yields, using
x,y^{w}\in[-A,A]
and
h_{T}^{w}\leq 1
for large enough
T
,

\left|\frac{h_{T}^{w}(x-y^{w^{\prime}})-h_{T}^{w^{\prime}}(x-y^{w})}{h_{T}^{w^{\prime}}h_{T}^{w}}\right|\leq\frac{R+2A}{h_{T}^{w}h_{T}^{w^{\prime}}}\left|h_{T}^{w}-h_{T}^{w^{\prime}}\right|.

Consequently, equation (
H.8
) yields for
\beta\leq 1

\displaystyle\|b^{w}-b^{w^{\prime}}\|_{[-A,A]}\leq L\left|(h_{T}^{w})^{\beta}-(h_{T}^{w^{\prime}})^{\beta}\right|+\left(\frac{R+2A}{h_{T}^{w}}\right)^{\beta}\left|h_{T}^{w}-h_{T}^{w^{\prime}}\right|^{\beta}

and for
\beta>1

\displaystyle\|b^{w}-b^{w^{\prime}}\|_{[-A,A]}\leq L\hskip-1.42271pt\left|(h_{T}^{w})^{\beta}-(h_{T}^{w^{\prime}})^{\beta}\right|\hskip-1.42271pt+\hskip-1.42271pt\|K_{\beta}^{\prime}\|_{\infty}(h_{T}^{w^{\prime}})^{\beta-1}\frac{R+2A}{h_{T}^{w}}\left|h_{T}^{w}-h_{T}^{w^{\prime}}\right|.

In this last expression the term
(h_{T}^{w^{\prime}})^{\beta-1}
can be upper bounded by one for large
T
since
\beta-1>0
. Finally, let
\epsilon>0
. In both cases
\beta\leq 1
and
\beta>1
, we find
\delta=\delta(w)>0
by continuity of
w\mapsto h_{T}^{w}
such that for
|w-w^{\prime}|<\delta
,

\|b^{w}-b^{w^{\prime}}\|_{[-A,A]}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{4A\sigma^{-2}(1\vee C_{b_{0,\eta},\sigma})e^{2C\sigma^{-2}A(1+A)}},

which finalises the proof of continuity of
w\mapsto\exp\left(\int_{0}^{\cdot}2\sigma^{-2}b^{w}(u)du\right)
and
w\mapsto C_{b^{w}}
as the right-hand side of both (
H.6
) and (
H.7
) is bounded from above by
\epsilon
for
|w-w^{\prime}|<\delta
.

We turn to the second mapping in (
H.5
). We add zero and get

\displaystyle\left|c_{T}q_{b^{w}}(y^{w})-c_{T}q_{b^{w^{\prime}}}(y^{w^{\prime}})\right|

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq c_{T}\left|q_{b^{w}}(y^{w})-q_{b^{w}}(y^{w^{\prime}})\right|+c_{T}\left|q_{b^{w}}(y^{w^{\prime}})-q_{b^{w^{\prime}}}(y^{w^{\prime}})\right|.

For arbitrary
\epsilon>0
and appropriate
\delta>0
, the first summand is bounded by
\epsilon
for
|y^{w}-y^{w^{\prime}}|<\delta
, as evaluation of a continuous function is a continuous mapping. The second summand is trivially bounded by
\epsilon>0
whenever
\|q_{b^{w}}-q_{b^{w^{\prime}}}\|_{[-A,A]}<\frac{\epsilon}{c_{T}}
.
∎

Lemma H.4
(
[
9
]
, Lemma 3.6.4).

Let the drift functions
b_{0,\eta}
and
b^{w}
be given as in
(
H.2
)
and
(
H.3
)
and let
w\in[L_{*}/2,L^{*}]
. Then we have

\left\|\frac{q_{b^{w}}}{q_{b_{0,\eta}}}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\exp\left(4\sigma^{-2}L(h_{T}^{w})^{\beta}\|K_{T}^{\beta}\|_{L^{1}}\right)\leq\exp\left(c\left(\frac{\log T}{T}\right)^{\frac{\beta}{2\beta+1}}\right)

for a constant
c>0
not depending on
T
and
w
.

Next, we present the proof of the crucial Proposition
8.1
that was already presented in the sketch of proof of Theorem
5.1
in Section
8
.

By assumption, we have

1=\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}]=\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{i}\leq\epsilon m\}}]+\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{i}>\epsilon m\}}].

Together with the fact that
Z_{i}>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}Z_{i}-1\right|\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left(Z_{i}\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{i}\leq\epsilon m\}}-\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{i}\leq\epsilon m\}}]\right)\right.\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\left.\left.+\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left(Z_{i}\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{i}>\epsilon m\}}-\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{i}>\epsilon m\}}]\right)\right|\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 2.84544pt\leq\frac{1}{m}\textrm{Var}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}Z_{i}\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{i}\leq\epsilon m\}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\frac{1}{m}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left|Z_{i}\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{i}>\epsilon m\}}\right|+\left|\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{i}>\epsilon m\}}]\right|\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 2.84544pt\leq\frac{1}{m}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\textrm{Var}\left(Z_{i}\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{i}\leq\epsilon m\}}\right)+\sum_{i\neq j}\textrm{Cov}\left(Z_{i}\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{i}\leq\epsilon m\}},Z_{j}\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{j}\leq\epsilon m\}}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt+\frac{2}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{i}\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{i}>\epsilon m\}}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 2.84544pt=:\frac{1}{m}\left(A_{1}+A_{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+A_{3}.

Now, we treat each term
A_{i}
,
i=1,2,3
, separately. As a variance, the term
A_{1}+A_{2}
is non-negative and it suffices to find an upper bound for it without taking the absolute value. In particular, we may drop negative summands without double-checking that they are in absolute value smaller than the remaining positive ones. Using
\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}]=1
, for
A_{1}
a bound is straightforward as

A_{1}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{i}^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{i}\leq\epsilon m\}}\right]\leq\epsilon m\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{i}\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{i}\leq\epsilon m\}}\right]\leq\epsilon m\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}]=\epsilon m^{2}.

For the second term
A_{2}
, we first note that
\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{i}\leq\epsilon m\}}=1-\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{i}>\epsilon m\}}
and similarly
\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{i}\leq\epsilon m\}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{j}\leq\epsilon m\}}=1-A_{ij}
with

\displaystyle A_{ij}:=\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{i}\leq\epsilon m\}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{j}>\epsilon m\}}+\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{i}>\epsilon m\}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{j}\leq\epsilon m\}}+\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{i}>\epsilon m\}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{j}>\epsilon m\}}.

Now, we can start with

\displaystyle A_{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\neq j}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{i}Z_{j}\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{i}\leq\epsilon m\}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{j}\leq\epsilon m\}}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{i}\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{i}\leq\epsilon m\}}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{j}\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{j}\leq\epsilon m\}}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\neq j}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{i}Z_{j}(1-A_{ij})\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{i}(1-\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{i}>\epsilon m\}})\right]\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{j}(1-\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{j}>\epsilon m\}})\right]\right)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i\neq j}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{i}Z_{j}A_{ij}\right]+\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{i}>\epsilon m\}}]\mathbb{E}[Z_{j}\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{j}>\epsilon m\}}]\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\sum_{i\neq j}\left(\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{i}>\epsilon m\}}]+\mathbb{E}[Z_{j}\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{j}>\epsilon m\}}]\right)+\sum_{i\neq j}\left(\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}Z_{j}]-1\right).

As
Z_{i}>0
and
A_{ij}\geq 0
, the first summand is non-positive and by assumption,

\sum_{i\neq j}\left(\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}Z_{j}]-1\right)\leq m(m-1)(C_{0}-1),

as well as

\sum_{i\neq j}\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{i}>\epsilon m\}}]+\mathbb{E}[Z_{j}\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{j}>\epsilon m\}}]=2(m-1)\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{i}>\epsilon m\}}],

and we get

A_{2}\leq 2m\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{i}>\epsilon m\}}]+m^{2}(C_{0}-1).

Now we use the indicator in the last remaining expectation to estimate for arbitrary
0<\nu\leq 1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{i}>\epsilon m\}}]\leq(\epsilon m)^{-\nu}\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{i}^{1+\nu}\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{i}>\epsilon m\}}\right]\leq(\epsilon m)^{-\nu}\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{i}^{1+\nu}\right].

(H.9)

Altogether, we arrive at

A_{2}\leq 2\epsilon^{-\nu}m^{1-\nu}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{i}^{1+\nu}\right]+m^{2}(C_{0}-1).

Using
\sqrt{a+b+c}\leq\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{b}+\sqrt{c}
for
a,b,c\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m}(A_{1}+A_{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{m}\left(\epsilon m^{2}+2\epsilon^{-\nu}m^{1-\nu}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{i}^{1+\nu}\right]+m^{2}(C_{0}-1)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{m}\left(\sqrt{\epsilon}m+\left(2\epsilon^{-\nu}m^{1-\nu}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{i}^{1+\nu}\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+m\sqrt{C_{0}-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\epsilon}+\left(2\epsilon^{-\nu}m^{-(1+\nu)}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{i}^{1+\nu}\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\sqrt{C_{0}-1},

which are exactly the first to summands in the assertion of the proposition. We close the proof by the estimation of
A_{3}
. But here we simply use the same trick with
0<\nu\leq 1
from (
H.9
) and directly get

A_{3}\leq 2\epsilon^{-\nu}m^{-(1+\nu)}\sum_{k=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{i}^{1+\nu}\right].

∎

Remark H.5
.

Proposition
8.1
is an extension of a similar result proven by Dümbgen and Walther in
[
20
]
(within the proof of Lemma
7.4
on p.
1777
) and avoids the assumption of independent random variables. If we additionally assume that the random variables
Z_{i}
are uncorrelated, we have
\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}Z_{j}]=1
for all
1\leq i<j\leq m
and the term
\sqrt{C_{0}-1}
vanishes. Even this makes the argument trickier as the uncorrelatedness does not carry over to truncated versions of the random variables.

We begin by building suitable hypotheses. Denote the support of the optimal recovery kernel solving (
5.3
) by
[-R,R]
, in particular
R=1
for
\beta\leq 1
. Then choose
b_{0}\in\Sigma\left(\frac{C}{2}-\eta,A,\gamma+\frac{\eta}{\sigma^{2}},\sigma\right)
for which
b_{0,\eta}=b_{0}+\eta\in H_{0}(b_{0},\eta)
and remember
h_{T}^{w}
defined in (
H.1
),
b^{w}
defined in (
H.3
) and
A^{\prime}=A-h_{T}^{L_{*}}
. Setting
c_{T}:=K_{T}^{\beta}(0)^{(2\beta+1)/\beta}
, we proceed inductively:

•

In the first step we set
y_{1}^{w}:=-A^{\prime}+Rh_{T}^{w}
. Then by Lemma
8.2
there exists
w_{1}>0
with

w_{1}=c_{T}q_{b^{w_{1}}}(y_{1}^{w_{1}})

and we set

b_{1}:=b^{w_{1}},\hskip 10.00002pty_{1}:=y_{1}^{w_{1}}.

The support of
b_{1}-b_{0,\eta}
is given by the interval
[-A^{\prime},-A^{\prime}+2Rh_{T}^{w_{1}}]=[y_{1}-Rh_{T}^{w_{1}},y_{1}+Rh_{T}^{w_{1}}]
.

•

If
b_{j}
is constructed with
y_{j}
we put
y_{j+1}^{w}:=y_{j}+Rh_{T}^{w_{j}}+Rh_{T}^{w}
. Again, Lemma
8.2
ensures existence of
w_{j+1}>0
with

w_{j+1}=c_{T}q_{b^{w_{j+1}}}(y_{j+1}^{w_{j+1}})

and we set

b_{j+1}:=b^{w_{j+1}},\hskip 10.00002pty_{j+1}:=y_{j+1}^{w_{j+1}}.

The support of
b_{j+1}-b_{0,\eta}
is given by
[y_{j}+Rh_{T}^{w_{j}},y_{j}+Rh_{T}^{w_{j}}+2Rh_{T}^{w_{j+1}}]=[y_{j+1}-Rh_{T}^{w_{j+1}},y_{j+1}+Rh_{T}^{w_{j+1}}]
and hence is disjoint of the support of all
b_{j}-b_{0,\eta}
constructed before.

•

The construction ends when for the first time the right endpoint of the newly constructed interval
[y_{N}-Rh_{T}^{w_{N}},y_{N}+Rh_{T}^{w_{N}}]
is greater than
A^{\prime}
. If this right endpoint is smaller than
A
, we consider
b_{1},\dots,b_{N}
, otherwise, we only use
b_{1},\dots,b_{N-1}
.

In what follows, we assume that we constructed
N
hypotheses for notational simplicity. In fact, nothing changes if we only have
b_{1},\dots,b_{N-1}
in the last step of the construction process, as their number has the same order with respect to
T
.

-A
A
-A^{\prime}
A^{\prime}
y_{1}
y_{2}
y_{3}
h_{1}
h_{2}
h_{3}

Fig 4:
The construction principle of our hats illustrated with the kernel
K_{0.75}
. First, the red hat is constructed from
-A^{\prime}
, then the green one starting at
y_{1}+h_{1}
and finally the blue hat. One can see that depending on the solution
w
of each fixed point problem, the height and width of each hat varies.

We have
w_{j}\leq c_{T}L^{*}\leq L^{*}
for all
j=1,\dots,N
by Lemma
8.2
. Next, we specify the order of
N
that depends on
T
. From the construction of the hypotheses we have
2A\geq\sum_{j=1}^{N}2Rh_{T}^{w_{j}}
. Hence,

\displaystyle\frac{A}{R}\geq\sum_{j=1}^{N}h_{T}^{w_{j}}\geq\left(\frac{c^{*}}{L}\right)^{\frac{1}{\beta}}\left(\frac{\log T}{T}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\beta+1}}N\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{L^{*}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\beta+1}},

which is equivalent to

N=N_{T}\leq\frac{A}{R}\left(\frac{L^{*}}{\sigma^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\beta+1}}\left(\frac{L}{c^{*}}\right)^{\frac{1}{\beta}}\left(\frac{T}{\log T}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\beta+1}}.

Moreover, we know by construction that
A\leq\sum_{j=1}^{N}2Rh_{T}^{w_{j}}
and the same computation shows with upper bounding
w_{j}^{-1}\leq(c_{T}L_{*})^{-1}\leq 2(L_{*})^{-1}
for
T
large enough, that

\frac{A}{2R}\leq\left(\frac{c^{*}}{L}\right)^{\frac{1}{\beta}}\left(\frac{\log T}{T}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\beta+1}}N\left(\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{L_{*}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\beta+1}},

or equivalently,

N=N_{T}\geq\frac{A}{2R}\left(\frac{L_{*}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\beta+1}}\left(\frac{L}{c^{*}}\right)^{\frac{1}{\beta}}\left(\frac{T}{\log T}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\beta+1}}.

Hence, we know the order of
N_{T}
.

For the hypothesis
b_{k}
, we have by construction
\Delta_{J}(b_{k})=(1-\epsilon_{T})c_{*}\delta_{T}
and in combination with Lemma
H.3
that
b_{k}\in H_{1}(b_{0},\eta)
for
k=1,\dots,N
. By Lemma
H.2
,
b_{k}-b_{0}\in\mathcal{H}(\beta,L)
for
k=1,\dots,N
. Then we have for any test
\psi
with
\sup_{b\in H_{0}(b_{0},\eta)}\mathbb{E}_{b}[\psi]\leq\alpha
,

\displaystyle\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in H_{1}(b_{0},\eta)\cap\{b-b_{0}\in\mathcal{H}(\beta,L)\}:\\
\Delta_{J}(b)\geq(1-\epsilon_{T})c_{*}\delta_{T}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}_{b}[\psi]-\alpha

\displaystyle\leq\min_{1\leq k\leq N}\mathbb{E}_{b_{k}}[\psi]-\alpha

\displaystyle\leq\min_{1\leq k\leq N}\mathbb{E}_{b_{k}}[\psi]-\sup_{b\in H_{0}(b_{0},\eta)}\mathbb{E}_{b}[\psi]

\displaystyle\leq\min_{1\leq k\leq N}\mathbb{E}_{b_{k}}[\psi]-\mathbb{E}_{b_{0,\eta}}[\psi]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{b_{0,\eta}}\left[\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{d\mathbb{P}_{b_{k}}}{d\mathbb{P}_{b_{0,\eta}}}(X)-1\right)\psi\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{b_{0,\eta}}\left[\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{d\mathbb{P}_{b_{k}}}{d\mathbb{P}_{b_{0,\eta}}}(X)-1\right|\right].

Note that we used in the third step that
b_{0,\eta}\in H_{0}(b_{0},\eta)
.
For ease of notation we introduce

Z_{k}:=\frac{d\mathbb{P}_{b_{k}}}{d\mathbb{P}_{b_{0,\eta}}}(X),

which is given by Girsanov’s theorem as

\frac{q_{b_{k}}(X_{0})}{q_{b_{0,\eta}}(X_{0})}\exp\left(\int_{0}^{T}A_{T,k}K_{T,k}(X_{s})dW_{s}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}A_{T,k}^{2}K_{T,k}(X_{s})^{2}ds\right),

where we used the notation

\displaystyle A_{T,k}:=\frac{(1-\epsilon_{T})L\left(h_{T}^{w_{k}}\right)^{\beta}}{\sigma}\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{ and }\hskip 10.00002ptK_{T,k}(\cdot):=K_{y_{k},h_{T}^{w_{k}}}(\cdot).

(H.10)

In the expression
K_{y_{k},h_{T}^{w_{k}}}(\cdot)
we use the rescaled version of the kernel
K_{T}^{\beta}
, which is suppressed in the notation
K_{T,k}
. The dependence on
\beta
is also not made explicit in this notation, but of course should be kept in mind.
As
Z_{k}>0
and
\mathbb{E}_{b_{0,\eta}}[Z_{k}]=1
for each
1\leq k\leq N
, we now apply Proposition
8.1
. To get

\mathbb{E}_{b_{0,\eta}}\left[\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{d\mathbb{P}_{b_{k}}}{d\mathbb{P}_{b_{0,\eta}}}(X)-1\right|\right]\rightarrow 0

for
T\to\infty
from this Proposition
8.1
, it remains to show that for all
\epsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\epsilon^{-\nu_{T}}N_{T}^{-(1+\nu_{T})}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{T}}\mathbb{E}_{b_{0}}\left[Z_{k}^{1+\nu_{T}}\right]\rightarrow 0\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt\sqrt{C_{T}-1}\rightarrow 0

(H.11)

for a suitable choice of
0<\nu=\nu_{T}\leq 1
and
T\to\infty
, where
C_{T}
is an upper bound of
\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}Z_{j}]
for all
1\leq i<j\leq N
. The latter condition means that
Z_{i}
and
Z_{j}
,
i\neq j
, are asymptotically uncorrelated.

We start with the second condition in (
H.11
) about
C_{T}
and estimate
\mathbb{E}_{b_{0,\eta}}[Z_{i}Z_{j}]
for
i\neq j
. In this case we have
K_{T,i}(X_{s})K_{T,j}(X_{s})=0
as the supports of
K_{T,i}
and
K_{T,j}
are disjoint. This will be used in the third step below. Moreover, we control the maximal value of the fraction of the invariant densities with Lemma
H.4
, where
a_{T}=\left(\log T/T\right)^{\beta/(2\beta+1)}
and
c>0
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{b_{0,\eta}}[Z_{i}Z_{j}]

\displaystyle\hskip 8.5359pt=\mathbb{E}_{b_{0,\eta}}\left[\frac{q_{b_{i}}(X_{0})}{q_{b_{0,\eta}}(X_{0})}\exp\left(\int_{0}^{T}A_{T,i}K_{T,i}(X_{s})dW_{s}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}A_{T,i}^{2}K_{T,i}^{2}(X_{s})^{2}ds\right)\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 34.14322pt\cdot\left.\frac{q_{b_{j}}(X_{0})}{q_{b_{0,\eta}}(X_{0})}\exp\left(\int_{0}^{T}A_{T,j}K_{T,j}(X_{s})dW_{s}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}A_{T,j}^{2}K_{T,j}(X_{s})^{2}ds\right)\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 8.5359pt\leq e^{2ca_{T}}\mathbb{E}_{b_{0,\eta}}\left[\exp\left(\int_{0}^{T}\left(A_{T_{i}}K_{T,i}(X_{s})+A_{T,j}K_{T,j}(X_{s})\right)dW_{s}\right.\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt\left.\left.-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\left(A_{T,i}^{2}K_{T,i}(X_{s})^{2}+A_{T,j}^{2}K_{T,j}(X_{s})^{2}\right)ds\right)\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 8.5359pt=e^{2ca_{T}}\mathbb{E}_{b_{0,\eta}}\left[\exp\left(\int_{0}^{T}\left(A_{T,i}K_{T,i}(X_{s})+A_{T,j}K_{T,j}(X_{s})\right)dW_{s}\right.\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt\left.\left.-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\left(A_{T,i}K_{T,i}(X_{s})+A_{T,j}K_{T,j}(X_{s})\right)^{2}ds\right)\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 8.5359pt=e^{2ca_{T}}\mathbb{E}_{b_{0,\eta}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{b_{0,\eta}}\left[\exp\left(\int_{0}^{T}\left(A_{T,i}K_{T,i}(X_{s})+A_{T,j}K_{T,j}(X_{s})\right)dW_{s}\right.\right.\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt\left.\left.\left.\left.-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\left(A_{T,i}K_{T,i}(X_{s})+A_{T,j}K_{T,j}(X_{s})\right)^{2}ds\right)\ \right|X_{0}\right]\right].

The last expression within the expectation is again a Girsanov-type density of the diffusions started in
X_{0}
. By boundedness of
A_{T,i}K_{T,i}
it follows that Novikov’s condition (cf.
[
27
]
, Theorem
19.24
) holds for the martingale

\left(\int_{0}^{t}\left(A_{T,i}K_{T,i}(X_{s})+A_{T,j}K_{T,j}(X_{s})\right)dW_{s}\right)_{t\in[0,T]}

and hence its stochastic exponential, which coincides with the density process, is a uniformly integrable martingale. In particular, its expectation equals one. Consequently,
\mathbb{E}_{b_{0,\eta}}[Z_{i}Z_{j}]\leq e^{2ca_{T}}
. Note that this bound is
\geq 1
and we have
\sqrt{e^{2ca_{T}}-1}\rightarrow 0
for
T\to\infty
, as desired.

The rest of the proof is dedicated to show the first convergence in (
H.11
). In a first step, we investigate one single summand
\mathbb{E}_{b_{0,\eta}}[Z_{k}^{1+\nu}]
that is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}_{b_{0,\eta}}\left[\left(\frac{q_{b_{k}}(X_{0})}{q_{b_{0,\eta}}(X_{0})}\right)^{1+\nu}\exp\left((1+\nu)\int_{0}^{T}A_{T,k}K_{T,k}(X_{s})dW_{s}\right.\right.\\
&\hskip 142.26378pt\left.\left.-\frac{1+\nu}{2}\int_{0}^{T}A_{T,k}^{2}K_{T,k}(X_{s})^{2}ds\right)\right].\end{split}

(H.12)

We now split the second integral in the exponent and get with the occupation times formula

\displaystyle-\frac{1+\nu}{2}\int_{0}^{T}A_{T,k}^{2}K_{T,k}(X_{s})^{2}ds

\displaystyle=-\frac{(1+\nu)^{2}}{2}\hskip-1.42271pt\int_{0}^{T}A_{T,k}^{2}K_{T,k}(X_{s})^{2}ds+\frac{T}{2}\nu(1+\nu)\hskip-1.42271pt\int_{\mathbb{R}}A_{T,k}^{2}K_{T,k}(z)^{2}q_{b_{0,\eta}}(y_{k})dz

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\frac{T}{2}\nu(1+\nu)\int_{\mathbb{R}}A_{T,k}^{2}K_{T,k}(z)^{2}\left(\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}T}L_{T}^{z}(X)-q_{b_{0,\eta}}(z)\right)dz

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\frac{T}{2}\nu(1+\nu)\int_{\mathbb{R}}A_{T,k}^{2}K_{T,k}(z)^{2}\left(q_{b_{0,\eta}}(z)-q_{b_{0,\eta}}(y_{k})\right)dz.

Inserting into the formula for
\mathbb{E}_{b_{0}}[Z_{k}^{1+\nu}]
in (
H.12
), pulling the non-random factors out of the integral and bounding the fraction of the densities leads to

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{b_{0,\eta}}[Z_{k}^{1+\nu}]\leq A_{1}\cdot A_{2}\cdot A_{3}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{b_{0,\eta}}\left[\exp\left((1+\nu)\int_{0}^{T}A_{T,k}K_{T,k}(X_{s})dW_{s}\right.\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt-\frac{(1+\nu)^{2}}{2}\int_{0}^{T}A_{T,k}^{2}K_{T,k}(X_{s})^{2}ds

\displaystyle\hskip 62.59596pt\left.\left.+\frac{T}{2}\nu(1+\nu)\int_{\mathbb{R}}A_{T,k}^{2}K_{T,k}(z)^{2}\left(\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}T}L_{T}^{z}(X)-q_{b_{0,\eta}}(z)\right)dz\right)\right],

where

\displaystyle A_{1}

\displaystyle:=\exp\left(\frac{T}{2}\nu(1+\nu)\int_{\mathbb{R}}A_{T,k}^{2}K_{T,k}(z)^{2}q_{b_{0,\eta}}(y_{k})dz\right),

\displaystyle A_{2}

\displaystyle:=\exp\left(\frac{T}{2}\nu(1+\nu)\int_{\mathbb{R}}A_{T,k}^{2}K_{T,k}(z)^{2}\left(q_{b_{0,\eta}}(z)-q_{b_{0,\eta}}(y_{k})\right)dz\right),\ \ \textrm{ and }

\displaystyle A_{3}

\displaystyle:=\left\|q_{b_{k}}/q_{b_{0,\eta}}\right\|_{\mathbb{R}}^{1+\nu}.

To further evaluate the term within the expectation, we again add a suitable zero. Therefore, pick
p=p_{T}>1
that will be specified later and the corresponding
q=q_{T}
with
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
. Then we rewrite the expectation in the following form:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{b_{0,\eta}}\hskip-1.42271pt\left[\exp\left(\hskip-1.42271pt(1+\nu)\hskip-1.42271pt\int_{0}^{T}\hskip-1.42271ptA_{T,k}K_{T,k}(X_{s})dW_{s}-\frac{p(1+\nu)^{2}}{2}\hskip-1.42271pt\int_{0}^{T}A_{T,k}^{2}K_{T,k}(X_{s})^{2}ds\right)\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}(p-1)(1+\nu)^{2}\int_{0}^{T}A_{T,k}^{2}K_{T,k}(X_{s})^{2}ds\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt\left.\left.+\frac{T}{2}\nu(1+\nu)\int_{\mathbb{R}}A_{T,k}^{2}K_{T,k}(z)^{2}\left(\frac{1}{T}L_{T}^{z}(X)-q_{b_{0,\eta}}(z)\right)dz\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{b_{0,\eta}}\left[\exp\left((1+\nu)\int_{0}^{T}A_{T,k}K_{T,k}(X_{s})dW_{s}\right.\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\left.\left.-\frac{p(1+\nu)^{2}}{2}\int_{0}^{T}A_{T,k}^{2}K_{T,k}(X_{s})^{2}ds\right)\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\cdot\exp\left(\frac{T}{2}(p-1)(1+\nu)^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}A_{T,k}^{2}K_{T,k}(z)^{2}q_{b_{0,\eta}}(z)dz\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt\left.\left.+\left(\frac{T}{2}(p-1)(1+\nu)^{2}+\frac{T}{2}\nu(1+\nu)\right)\right.\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 128.0374pt\left.\left.\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}}A_{T,k}^{2}K_{T,k}(z)^{2}\left(\frac{1}{T}L_{T}^{z}(X)-q_{b_{0,\eta}}(z)\right)dz\right)\right].

Here, we first applied the occupation times formula and then splitted
\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}T}L_{T}^{z}(X)=q_{b_{0,\eta}}(z)+\left(\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}T}L_{T}^{z}(X)-q_{b_{0,\eta}}(z)\right)
. Pulling the non-random factor out of the expectation and applying Hölder’s inequality yields

\mathbb{E}_{b_{0,\eta}}[Z_{k}^{1+\nu}]=A_{1}\cdot A_{2}\cdot A_{3}\cdot A_{4}\cdot A_{5}\cdot A_{6},

where

\displaystyle A_{4}

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}_{b_{0,\eta}}\left[\exp\left(p(1+\nu)\int_{0}^{T}A_{T,k}K_{T,k}(X_{s})dW_{s}\right.\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\left.\left.-\frac{p^{2}(1+\nu)^{2}}{2}\int_{0}^{T}A_{T,k}^{2}K_{T,k}(X_{s})^{2}ds\right)\right]^{\frac{1}{p}},

\displaystyle A_{5}

\displaystyle:=\exp\left(\frac{T}{2}(p-1)(1+\nu)^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}A_{T,k}^{2}K_{T,k}(z)^{2}q_{b_{0,\eta}}(z)dz\right),\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{ and }

\displaystyle A_{6}

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}_{b_{0,\eta}}\left[\exp\left(q\left(\frac{T}{2}(p-1)(1+\nu)^{2}+\frac{T}{2}\nu(1+\nu)\right)\right.\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt\left.\left.\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}}A_{T,k}^{2}K_{T,k}(z)^{2}\left(\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}T}L_{T}^{z}(X)-q_{b_{0,\eta}}(z)\right)dz\right)\right]^{\frac{1}{q}}.

The most interesting term will be
A_{1}
and we consider it later within the sum (remember that we still just treat
Z_{k}^{1+\nu}
for some fixed
k
here). The terms
A_{2},\dots,A_{6}
on the other side will turn out to be bounded from above by constants independent of
k
. We will use the definition of
A_{T,k}
and
K_{T,k}
from (
H.10
) in the following estimates without further notice:

A_{2}:

According to Lemma
D.1
, the invariant density is Lipschitz continuous with constant
L^{*}
, and we have
|q_{b_{0,\eta}}(y_{k})-q_{b_{0,\eta}}(z)|\leq 2L^{*}h_{T}^{w_{k}}
on the support of
K_{T,k}
. Hence,

\displaystyle A_{2}

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\nu(1+\nu)TA_{T,k}^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{T,k}(z)^{2}\left(q_{b_{0,\eta}}(z)-q_{b_{0,\eta}}(y_{k})\right)dz\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\nu(1+\nu)TA_{T,k}^{2}2L^{*}(h_{T}^{w_{k}})^{2}\|K_{T}^{\beta}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(2\|K_{\beta}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}L^{*}L^{2}(1-\epsilon_{T})^{2}\left(\frac{c_{*}}{L}\right)^{\frac{2\beta+1}{\beta}}Th_{T}^{w_{k}}\frac{\log T}{Tw_{k}}\right).

The last step used
\nu\leq 1
. This upper bound of
A_{2}
converges to one, as the exponent goes to zero due to the fact that
w_{k}
is bounded from below by
L_{*}/2
for
T
large enough,
(1-\epsilon_{T})\rightarrow 1
and
h_{T}^{w_{k}}\log T\rightarrow 0
as for some
\tilde{c}>0
,

h_{T}^{w_{k}}\log T\leq\tilde{c}\left(\frac{\log T}{T}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\beta+1}}\log T=T^{-\frac{1}{2\beta+1}}\left(\log T\right)^{\frac{2\beta+2}{2\beta+1}}.

A_{3}:

Let
T
be large enough such that
c_{T}=K_{T}^{\beta}(0)^{(2\beta+1)/\beta}\geq\frac{1}{2}
. Then by Lemma
H.4
and
\nu\leq 1
,

A_{3}\leq\exp\left(2c\left(\frac{\log T}{T}\right)^{\frac{\beta}{2\beta+1}}\right),

where
c
does not depend on
T
and
k
. This term obviously tends to one for
T\to\infty
.

A_{4}:

For every
T>0
, the term
A_{4}
is the expectation of a Girsanov-type density. By boundedness of the integrand
A_{T,k}K_{T,k}
, Novikov’s condition (cf.
[
27
]
, Theorem
19.24
) holds for the martingale

\left(p(1+\nu)\int_{0}^{t}A_{T,k}K_{T,k}(X_{s})dW_{s}\right)_{t\in[0,T]}

and hence its stochastic exponential, which coincides with the term inside the expectation in
A_{4}
, is a uniformly integrable martingale. In particular, its expectation equals one, i.e.
A_{4}=1
.

A_{5}:

Here, we use again Lemma
D.1
to get
\|q_{b_{0,\eta}}\|_{\infty}\leq L^{*}
. Then we estimate

\displaystyle A_{5}=\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}(p-1)(1+\nu)^{2}TA_{T,k}^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{T,k}(z)^{2}q_{b_{0,\eta}}(z)dz\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}(p-1)(1+\nu)^{2}L^{*}\|K_{T}^{\beta}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}TA_{T,k}^{2}h_{T}^{w_{k}}\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}(1+\nu)^{2}L^{*}\|K_{T}^{\beta}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}L^{2}\left(\frac{c_{*}}{L}\right)^{\frac{2\beta+1}{\beta}}w_{k}^{-1}(1-\epsilon_{T})^{2}T(p-1)\frac{\log T}{T}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(2L^{*}\|K_{\beta}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}L^{2}\left(\frac{c_{*}}{L}\right)^{\frac{2\beta+1}{\beta}}w_{k}^{-1}(1-\epsilon_{T})^{2}(p-1)\log T\right),

where we used
(1+\nu)\leq 2
in the last step. Remember that
\epsilon_{T}\to 0
and
w_{k}\in[L_{*}/2,L^{*}]
for
T
large enough. Consequently, the last exponent converges to zero if
(p-1)\log T\rightarrow 0
which is for example true, if we set

p=p_{T}=1+T^{-\frac{1}{4}}.

In particular, we have
p>1
, which is import as we applied the Hölder inequality for it. Of course, many other choices of
p
would be equally reasonable here, but we will see in the next step, that
p
must not be too close to one and our choice perfectly applies for both
A_{5}
and
A_{6}
.

A_{6}:

In this case we have

\displaystyle A_{6}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{b_{0,\eta}}\left[\exp\left(q\left(\frac{1}{2}(p-1)(1+\nu)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\nu(1+\nu)\right)TA_{T,k}^{2}\right.\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\left.\left.\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{T,k}(z)^{2}\left(\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}T}L_{T}^{z}(X)-q_{b_{0,\eta}}(z)\right)dz\right)\right]^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{b_{0,\eta}}\left[\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}(1+\nu)c_{p}\|K_{T}^{\beta}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}L^{2}\left(\frac{c_{*}}{L}\right)^{\frac{2\beta+1}{\beta}}w_{k}^{-1}\right.\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 108.12054pt\left.\left.\cdot(1-\epsilon_{T})^{2}\log T\left\|\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}T}L_{T}(X)-q_{b_{0,\eta}}\right\|_{\infty}\right)\right]^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{b_{0,\eta}}\left[\exp\left(\tilde{c}c_{p}\log T\left\|\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}T}L_{T}(X)-q_{b_{0,\eta}}\right\|_{\infty}\right)\right]^{\frac{1}{q}},

with

\displaystyle\tilde{c}

\displaystyle:=2\|K_{\beta}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}L^{2}\left(\frac{c_{*}}{L}\right)^{\frac{2\beta+1}{\beta}}L_{*}^{-1},

\displaystyle c_{p}

\displaystyle:=q((p-1)(1+\nu)+\nu).

In the estimation, we bounded
\nu\leq 1
and
\|K_{T}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq\|K_{\beta}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
to get rid of the dependence on
T
in the constant, used
(1-\epsilon_{T})^{2}\leq 1
and the bound
w_{k}\geq\frac{1}{2}L_{*}
for
T
large enough. Note further that
q
is determined by
p
as its conjugate. By a series expanison of
\exp(\cdot)
and monotone convergence as well as an application of Proposition
D.2
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{b_{0,\eta}}\left[\exp\left(\tilde{c}c_{p}\log T\left\|\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}T}L_{T}^{z}(X)-q_{b_{0,\eta}}(z)\right\|_{\infty}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(\tilde{c}c_{p}\log T)^{k}}{k!}\mathbb{E}_{b_{0,\eta}}\left[\left\|\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}T}L_{T}(X)-\rho_{b_{0,\eta}}\right\|_{\infty}^{k}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(\tilde{c}c_{p}\log T)^{k}}{k!}c_{1}^{k}\left(\frac{k}{T}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\left(1+\sqrt{k}+\sqrt{\log T}\right)+Te^{-c_{2}T}\right)^{k}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{k^{k}}{k!}(\tilde{c}c_{1})^{k}(c_{p}B_{T})^{k},

with

B_{T}=\frac{\log T}{T}+\frac{\log T}{\sqrt{T}}\left(2+\sqrt{\log T}\right)+T(\log T)e^{-c_{2}T}.

Considering the power series of
\exp(\cdot)
, we have
e^{x}>\frac{x^{n}}{n!}
for
x\geq 0
and hence for
x=n
,

\frac{n^{n}}{n!}<e^{n}.

Moreover, we remember
p=p_{T}=1+T^{-\frac{1}{4}}
from the previous step of bounding
A_{5}
. This choice gives
q=\frac{p}{p-1}=1+T^{\frac{1}{4}}
and with
\nu\leq 1
, we have for
T\geq 1
,

c_{p}=(1+T^{\frac{1}{4}})\left((1+\nu)T^{-\frac{1}{4}}+\nu\right)\leq 3(1+T^{\frac{1}{4}}).

Thus, after inserting all this, we end up with the upper bound

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{k^{k}}{k!}(\tilde{c}c_{1})^{k}(c_{p}B_{T})^{k}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(\tilde{c}c_{1}e)^{k}\left(3(1+T^{\frac{1}{4}})B_{T}\right)^{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(3\tilde{c}c_{1}e)^{k}\tilde{B}_{T}^{k},

where
\tilde{B}_{T}
is defined as

\frac{\log T(1+T^{\frac{1}{4}})}{T}+\frac{\log T(1+T^{\frac{1}{4}})}{\sqrt{T}}\left(2+\sqrt{\log T}\right)+T(1+T^{\frac{1}{4}})(\log T)e^{-c_{2}T}.

The important feature now is that
\tilde{B}_{T}\rightarrow 0
for
T\to\infty
and we have shown so far

A_{6}\leq\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(3\tilde{c}c_{1}e)^{k}\tilde{B}_{T}^{k}\right)^{\frac{1}{1+T^{1/4}}}.

Now it is time to choose
T
large enough such that
\tilde{B}_{T}<(3\tilde{c}c_{1}e)^{-1}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left(3\tilde{c}c_{1}e\tilde{B}_{T}\right)^{k}=\frac{1}{1-3\tilde{c}c_{1}e\tilde{B}_{T}}>1.

As
(1+T^{1/4})^{-1}<1
we get the bound

A_{6}\leq\frac{1}{1-3\tilde{c}c_{1}e\tilde{B}_{T}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle T\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}1.

Summing up, we have shown so far that there exists
T_{0}
and a constant
C^{\prime}=C^{\prime}(T_{0})>1
not depending on
k
such that for
T\geq T_{0}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{b_{0}}\left[Z_{k}^{1+\nu}\right]\leq C^{\prime}A_{1}=C^{\prime}\exp\left(\frac{T}{2}\nu(1+\nu)A_{T,k}^{2}q_{b_{0,\eta}}(y_{k})\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{T,k}(z)^{2}dz\right).

(H.13)

Next, we analyse the deterministic exponent and plug in the definitions of
A_{T,k}
,
K_{k}
from (
H.10
) and of the optimal constant
c_{*}
given in (
5.2
). This yields

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\nu(1+\nu)q_{b_{0,\eta}}(y_{k})TA_{T,k}^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{T,k}(z)^{2}dz

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt=\nu(1+\nu)\frac{1}{2\beta+1}\frac{\|K_{T}^{\beta}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}{\|K_{\beta}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}\frac{q_{b_{0,\eta}}(y_{k})}{w_{k}}(1-\epsilon_{T})^{2}\log T.

It remains to show that both the fraction of the invariant density and
w_{k}
and that of the
L^{2}
-norms are close enough to one. For the first one, things get easier, if we can express its order in
T
without dependence on
k
. First of all, we use that
w_{k}=q_{b_{k}}(y_{k})K_{T}^{\beta}(0)^{(2\beta+1)/\beta}
and hence,

\frac{q_{b_{0},\eta}(y_{k})}{w_{k}}=\frac{q_{b_{0,\eta}}(y_{k})}{q_{b_{k}}(y_{k})}K_{T}^{\beta}(0)^{-\frac{2\beta+1}{\beta}}=\left(1+\mathcal{O}(a_{T})\right)K_{T}^{\beta}(0)^{-\frac{2\beta+1}{\beta}},

where
a_{T}=(\log(T)/T)^{\beta/(2\beta+1)}
. This can be seen by following the lines of the proof of Lemma
H.4
. For
\beta\geq 1
we have
K_{T}^{\beta}(0)=1
, for
\beta<1
we have
K_{T}^{\beta}(0)=(1-T^{-\beta})
. By a Taylor expansion at
x=0
, we have for
\alpha>0
that
(1-x)^{-\alpha}=1+\mathcal{O}(x)
as
\alpha(1-x)^{-\alpha-1}
is bounded for
0\leq x\leq\frac{1}{2}
and hence for each
\beta>0
and
T
large enough such that
T^{-\beta}\leq\frac{1}{2}
,

K_{T}^{\beta}(0)^{-\frac{2\beta+1}{\beta}}=1+\mathcal{O}(T^{-\beta})\leq 1+\mathcal{O}(a_{T}).

We conclude

\frac{q_{b_{0,\eta}}(y_{k})}{w_{k}}=(1+\mathcal{O}(a_{T}))^{2}=1+\mathcal{O}(a_{T}).

For the
L^{2}
-norms we write

\frac{\|K_{T}^{\beta}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}{\|K_{\beta}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}=\frac{\|K_{\beta}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|K_{T}^{\beta}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}-\|K_{\beta}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}{\|K_{\beta}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}=1+\mathcal{O}(T^{-\frac{1}{2}-\beta})

by Lemma
H.1
. Now, every ingredient is prepared and we finalize the proof. Remember that
N=N_{T}\geq c\left(T/\log T\right)^{\frac{1}{2\beta+1}}
for some constant
c>0
. Then we have from (
H.13
) that

\displaystyle N^{-(1+\nu)}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}_{b_{0,\eta}}\left[Z_{k}^{1+\nu}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 2.84544pt\leq C^{\prime}N^{-\nu}\exp\left(\nu(1+\nu)\frac{1}{2\beta+1}(1+\mathcal{O}(a_{T}))(1+\mathcal{O}(T^{-\frac{1}{2}-\beta}))(1-\epsilon_{T})^{2}\log T\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 2.84544pt=C^{\prime}\exp\left(\nu(1+\nu)\frac{1}{2\beta+1}(1+\mathcal{O}(a_{T}))(1+\mathcal{O}(T^{-\frac{1}{2}-\beta}))\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 213.39566pt\cdot\left.(1-\epsilon_{T})^{2}\log T-\nu\log N\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 2.84544pt\leq C^{\prime}\exp\left(\nu(1+\nu)\frac{1}{2\beta+1}(1+\mathcal{O}(a_{T}))(1+\mathcal{O}(T^{-\frac{1}{2}-\beta}))(1-\epsilon_{T})^{2}\log T\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt\left.-\nu\frac{1}{2\beta+1}\left(\log T-\log\log T\right)-\nu\log c\right).

We note
(1+\mathcal{O}(a_{T}))(1+\mathcal{O}(T^{-\frac{1}{2}-\beta}))=1+\mathcal{O}(a_{T}\vee T^{-\frac{1}{2}-\beta})
. Setting
\nu=\epsilon_{T}
we have
(\nu(1+\nu)(1-\epsilon_{T})^{2}=\epsilon_{T}-\epsilon_{T}^{2}-\epsilon_{T}^{3}+\epsilon_{T}^{4}
and hence the last expression equals

\displaystyle C^{\prime}\exp\left(\frac{1}{2\beta+1}\left(\left(\epsilon_{T}-\epsilon_{T}^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon_{T}^{3})\right)(1+\mathcal{O}(a_{T}\vee T^{-\frac{1}{2}-\beta}))-\epsilon_{T}\right)\log T\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\left.+\frac{1}{2\beta+1}\epsilon_{T}\log\log T-\epsilon_{T}\log c\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=C^{\prime}\exp\left(\frac{1}{2\beta+1}\left(-\epsilon_{T}^{2}(1+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon_{T}))+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon_{T})\mathcal{O}(a_{T}\vee T^{-\frac{1}{2}-\beta})\right)\log T\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\left.+\frac{1}{2\beta+1}\epsilon_{T}\log\log T-\epsilon_{T}\log c\right).

Looking after the definition of
a_{T}
we see that
\mathcal{O}(a_{T}\vee T^{-\frac{1}{2}-\beta})\log T=o_{T}(1)
and from the choice of
\epsilon_{T}
we also get
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon_{T})\mathcal{O}(a_{T}\vee T^{-\frac{1}{2}-\beta})\log T=o_{T}(1)
. It is clear by
\epsilon_{T}\to 0
that
\epsilon_{T}\log c=o_{T}(1)
. Furthermore,

\displaystyle-\epsilon_{T}^{2}(1+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon_{T}))\log T+\epsilon_{T}\log\log T

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=-\epsilon_{T}^{2}\log T\left(1+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon_{T})-\frac{\log\log T}{\epsilon_{T}\log T}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=-\epsilon_{T}^{2}\log T\left(1+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon_{T})-\frac{\log\log T}{\epsilon_{T}\sqrt{\log T}\cdot\sqrt{\log T}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=-\epsilon_{T}^{2}\log T\left(1+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon_{T})+o_{T}(1)\right),

where the last step used
\epsilon_{T}\sqrt{\log T}\rightarrow\infty
by the choice of
\epsilon_{T}
. Using this again, this last term tends to
-\infty
and thus,

\displaystyle C^{\prime}\exp\left(\frac{1}{2\beta+1}\left(-\epsilon_{T}^{2}(1+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon_{T}))+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon_{T})\mathcal{O}(a_{T}\vee b_{T})\right)\log T\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\left.+\frac{1}{2\beta+1}\epsilon_{T}\log\log T-\epsilon_{T}\log c\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle T\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}\ 0.

In conclusion, the first convergence in (
H.11
) holds true and the theorem is proven.
∎

Remark H.6
.

We have discussed in Remark
5.5
how our results can be transferred from the stationary case
X_{0}\sim\mu_{b}
to a diffusion started at
X_{0}=x_{0}\in[-A,A]
. For the lower bound in Theorem
5.1
, Proposition
D.2
is used with the explicit moment bound, which is also available in the case
X_{0}=x_{0}
, see Remark
D.3
. Moreover, in the evaluation of the likelihood ratio term
\mathbb{P}_{b_{k}}/\mathbb{P}_{b_{0,\eta}}
, the stationarity assumption occurs as the likelihood of the initial values. But this term drops out in the case
X_{0}=x_{0}
, making the proof even slightly easier, as the factor
A_{3}
equals one. In particular, Theorem
5.1
remains valid in the non-stationary case. This is used for our comparison with the fractional diffusion model in Section
6
, but is of course of independent interest.

H.2
The proofs of the minimax upper bounds in Section
5

In this subsection, we briefly sketch the proof of Theorem
5.2
. Although it requires sound knowledge of stochastic calculus, the principle ideas do not differ substantially from similar other minimax upper bounds for nonparametric tests. A complete version of this proof can be found in Section
3.7
of
[
9
]
, together with detailed proofs of Theorem
5.3
and
5.4
.
Subsequently we write
X_{T}=o_{\mathbb{P}_{b},\textrm{unif}}(1)
if
X_{T}
converges uniformly for
b\in\Sigma(C,A,\gamma,\sigma)
to zero in probability, i.e. for all
\epsilon>0
,

\lim_{T\to\infty}\sup_{b\in\Sigma(C,A,\gamma,\sigma)}\mathbb{P}_{b}\left(\left|X_{T}\right|>\epsilon\right)=0.

Moreover, we denote this uniform stochastic convergence by
\rightarrow_{\mathbb{P}_{b},\textrm{unif}}
.

The start of the proof is the following observation: For every
y\in J
the probability
\mathbb{P}_{b}(T_{T}^{\eta}(X)>\kappa_{\eta,\alpha})
of rejecting the null hypothesis is bounded from below by

\mathbb{P}_{b}\left(|\Psi_{T,y,h}^{b_{0}}(X)|>\kappa_{\eta,\alpha}+\Upsilon\left(\hat{\sigma}_{T}(y,h)^{2}/\hat{\sigma}_{T,\max}^{2}\right)+\Lambda_{T,y,h}^{\eta}(X)\right),

which in turn is bounded from below by

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{b}\left(-\textrm{sign}\left(\int_{0}^{T}K_{y,h}(X_{s})\left(b(X_{s})-b_{0}(X_{s})\right)ds\right)\frac{\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\int_{0}^{T}K_{y,h}(X_{s})dW_{s}}{\sqrt{\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}K_{y,h}(X_{s})^{2}ds}}\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt<-\kappa_{\eta,\alpha}-\Upsilon\left(\frac{\hat{\sigma}_{T}(y,h)^{2}}{\hat{\sigma}_{T,\max}^{2}}\right)-\Lambda_{T,y,h}^{\eta}(X)

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\left.+\frac{\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\left|\int_{0}^{T}K_{y,h}(X_{s})\left(b(X_{s})-b_{0}(X_{s})\right)ds\right|}{\sigma\sqrt{\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}K_{y,h}(X_{s})^{2}ds}}\right).

The idea of the proof is to proceed as follows: First, we show that the left-hand side of the inequality forms an asymptotically tight sequence (uniformly in
b
). Then, the claim is established in a second step in which it is shown that

\displaystyle\frac{\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\left|\int_{0}^{T}K_{y,h}(X_{s})\left(b_{T}(X_{s})-b_{0}(X_{s})\right)ds\right|-\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{T}}\int_{0}^{T}K_{y,h}(X_{s})ds}{\sigma\sqrt{\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}K_{y,h}(X_{s})^{2}ds}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-\Upsilon\left(\frac{\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}K_{y,h}(X_{s})^{2}ds}{\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbbm{1}_{[-A,A]}(X_{s})ds}\right)\longrightarrow_{\mathbb{P}_{b},\textrm{unif}}\ \infty

for all
b_{T}\in H_{1}(b_{0},\eta)\cap\{b-b_{0}\in\mathcal{H}(\beta,L)\}
with
\Delta(b_{T})\geq(1+\epsilon_{T})c\delta_{T}
and some
(y,h)=(y_{T},h_{T})
, where
c
is specified appropriately. For
\beta\leq 1
, this convergence follows from the lower bound

c_{*}^{\frac{2\beta+1}{2\beta}}L^{-\frac{1}{2\beta}}\|K_{\beta}\|_{L^{2}}\sqrt{\log T}(1+\epsilon_{T})^{\frac{2\beta+1}{2\beta}}q_{b_{T}}(y_{T})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\frac{q_{b_{T}}(y_{T})-a_{T}^{b_{T}}(y_{T})}{\sqrt{q_{b_{T}}(y_{T})+a_{T}^{b_{T}}(y_{T})}}

of the first summand, where
K_{\beta}
denots the optimal recovery kernel and

a_{T}^{b_{T}}(y_{T}):=\left\|(\sigma^{2}T)^{-1}L_{T}^{z}(X)-q_{b_{T}}(y)\right\|_{[y_{T}-h_{T},y_{T}+h_{T}]},

together with the fact that

\Upsilon\left(\frac{\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}K_{y_{T},h_{T}}(X_{s})^{2}ds}{\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbbm{1}_{[-A,A]}(X_{s})ds}\right)=\sqrt{\frac{2}{2\beta+1}\log T}+o_{\mathbb{P}_{b},\textrm{unif}}(1).

The case
\beta>1
can be treated similarly with a non-accurate constant in the lower bound. The reason for this is that we cannot work with the optimal recovery kernel for
\beta>1
due to the fact that
K_{\beta}
can take negative values in this case.
∎

Remark H.7
.

The results in Theorem
5.2
,
5.3
and
5.4
and their auxiliary results (in particular Theorem
7.1
) were derived under the assumption that the diffusion
X
is started in the invariant density, i.e.
X_{0}\sim\mu_{b}
. As outlined in Remark
D.3
, the moment inequality for the deviation of the normalized local time and invariant density given in Proposition
D.2
remains valid under the assumption that
X_{0}=x_{0}\in[-A,A]
is fixed. Our results used stationarity, i.e.
X_{0}\sim\mu_{b}
, only via Proposition
D.2
and hence, Theorem
5.2
,
5.3
and
5.4
remain valid if the diffusion
X
is started at a fixed point
X_{0}=x_{0}\in[-A,A]
.

A closer look reveals that one only needs the uniform stochastic convergence

\sup_{b\in\Sigma(C,A,\gamma,\sigma)}\mathbb{P}_{b}\left(\log T\left\|\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}T}L_{T}^{\cdot}(X)-q_{b}\right\|_{[-A,A]}>\epsilon\right)\rightarrow 0

to derive these theorems, which is a weaker statement than the moment inequality in Proposition
D.2
.

I
Proofs for Section
6

This Section contains all proofs of Section
6
. In Subsection
I.1
a proof of the continuity result in Theorem
6.1
is given. In Subsection
I.2
some details about fractional Brownian motion and the fractional diffusion model are given, in particular a Girsanov-type formula and some preliminaries on fractional calculus together with the proof of Proposition
6.2
. Those results are used in the subsequent Subsection
I.3
and
I.4
that contain the proofs of Theorem
6.3
and Theorem
6.5
.

I.1
Proof of Theorem
6.1

We begin with a preliminary lemma.

Lemma I.1
.

Let
s\in\mathcal{S}
be an element of a compact metric space
(\mathcal{S},d_{\mathcal{S}})
and
f:\mathcal{S}\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a function such that

(a)

|f|\leq c
is bounded,

(b)

f(s,\cdot)
is uniformly continuous with
\textrm{supp}(f(s,\cdot))\subset[-A,A]
for every
s\in\mathcal{S}
, and

(c)

s\mapsto f(s,y)
is continuous for almost all
y\in\mathbb{R}
.

Then for fixed
T>0
, the map

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}([0,T])\times\mathcal{S}\ \

\displaystyle\rightarrow\ \ \ \ \ \mathbb{R},

\displaystyle((x_{u})_{0\leq u\leq T},s)\ \

\displaystyle\mapsto\ \int_{0}^{T}f(s,x_{u})du,

is continuous in
(x,s)
for all
x\in\mathcal{C}([0,T])
and
s\in\mathcal{S}
, where the left-hand side is equipped with the product topology on
\mathcal{C}([0,T])\times\mathcal{S}
, where
\mathcal{C}([0,T])
is considered as a normed vector space with
\|\cdot\|_{[0,T]}
.

Let
\epsilon>0
. We have

\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{T}f(s,x_{u})du-\int_{0}^{T}f(s^{\prime},x_{u}^{\prime})du\right|

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\leq\left|\int_{0}^{T}f(s,x_{u})-f(s^{\prime},x_{u})du\right|+\left|\int_{0}^{T}f(s^{\prime},x_{u})-f(s^{\prime},x_{u}^{\prime})du\right|.

By dominated convergence and assumption (a) and (c) there exists a
\delta_{1}>0
such that

\left|\int_{0}^{T}f(s,x_{u})-f(s^{\prime},x_{u})du\right|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}

for
d_{\mathcal{S}}(s,s^{\prime})<\delta_{1}
.
By uniform continuity of
f(s,\cdot)
, for each
s\in\mathcal{S}
there exists
\delta_{s}>0
such that

\left|f(s,y)-f(s,y^{\prime})\right|<\frac{\epsilon}{2T}\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{ for all }\ |y-y^{\prime}|<\delta_{s}.

As
\mathcal{S}
is compact, we have
\delta_{2}:=\inf_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\delta_{s}>0
because the infimum is attained for some
s_{0}
. Then for
\|x-x^{\prime}\|_{[0,T]}<\delta_{2}
,

\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{T}f(s^{\prime},x_{u})-f(s^{\prime},x_{u}^{\prime})du\right|\leq T\sup_{u\in[0,T]}\left|f(s^{\prime},x_{u})-f(s^{\prime},x_{u}^{\prime})\right|<\frac{\epsilon}{2}.

Putting together both estimates, the claim follows.
∎

Denote

\overline{\mathcal{T}}_{T}:=\{(y,h)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\mid h_{\min}(T)\leq h\leq A\textrm{ and }-A+h\leq y\leq A-h\}.

With Lemma
I.1
, we establish continuity of the mapping

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{C}([0,T])\times\overline{\mathcal{T}}_{T}\ \ &\longrightarrow\ \ \ \ \ \ \mathbb{R},\\
((x_{s})_{s\in[0,T]},(y,h))\ &\ \mapsto\ \ \int_{0}^{T}f_{i}((y,h),x_{s})ds,\end{split}

(I.1)

for the functions
f_{1}((y,h),z):=K_{y,h}(z)^{2}
,
f_{2}((y,h),z):=K_{y,h}(z)b_{0}(z)
and
f_{3}((y,h),z):=\frac{1}{h}K^{\prime}((y-z)/h)
and additionally of

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{C}([0,T])\times\overline{\mathcal{T}}_{T}\ \ &\longrightarrow\ \ \ \ \ \ \mathbb{R},\\
((x_{s})_{s\in[0,T]},(y,h))\ \ &\ \mapsto\ \ \int_{x_{0}}^{x_{T}}K_{y,h}(z)dz.\end{split}

(I.2)

Then we can conclude that

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}([0,T])\times\overline{\mathcal{T}}_{T}\ \

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\ \ \ \ \mathbb{R},

\displaystyle((x_{s})_{s\in[0,T]},(y,h))\ \

\displaystyle\ \mapsto\ \ \tilde{\Psi}_{T,y,h}^{b_{0}}(x),

is continuous as a concatenation of continuous mappings and afterwards the claim follows by continuity of the map
\Upsilon(\cdot)
on
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, compactness of
\overline{\mathcal{T}}_{T}
, denseness of
\mathcal{T}_{T}\subset\overline{\mathcal{T}}_{T}
and the fact that
g(y):=\sup_{x\in X}f(x,y)
is continuous for continuous
f:X\times Y\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, where
X
and
Y
are metric spaces and
X
is compact (cf. Theorem
14.30
in
[
2
]
).

For continuity of (
I.1
), we first note that for
(y,h),(y^{\prime},h^{\prime})\in\overline{\mathcal{T}}_{T}
we have
K_{y,h}(x)\rightarrow K_{y^{\prime},h^{\prime}}(x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
in the limit
(y^{\prime},h^{\prime})\to(y,h)
by continuity of
K
and the same holds true for
K^{\prime}
. Therefore, for
f_{i}
,
i=1,2,3
, condition (c) of Lemma
I.1
is fullfilled and condition (b) is clear, as we work with continuous functions having compact support, which is a subset of
[-A,A]
. Moreover,
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are obviously bounded and
f_{3}
is bounded, as
h
is bounded away from zero on
\overline{\mathcal{T}}_{T}
, so condition (a) of Lemma
I.1
holds as well. Consequently, Lemma
I.1
is applicable and the mapping in (
I.1
) is continuous for
f_{i}
,
i=1,2,3
.

Lastly, we show continuity of (
I.2
) in
((x_{s})_{s\in[0,T]},(y,h))
and pick some
((x_{s}^{\prime})_{s\in[0,T]},(y^{\prime},h^{\prime}))
. Suppose
x_{0}\leq x_{T}
and
x_{0}^{\prime}\leq x_{T}^{\prime}
(the other cases work the same), then

\displaystyle\left|\int_{x_{0}}^{x_{T}}K_{y,h}(z)dz-\int_{x_{0}^{\prime}}^{x_{T}^{\prime}}K_{y^{\prime},h^{\prime}}(z)dz\right|

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=\left|\int_{x_{0}}^{x_{T}}K_{y,h}(z)-K_{y^{\prime},h^{\prime}}(z)dz+\int_{x_{0}}^{x_{T}}K_{y^{\prime},h^{\prime}}(z)dz-\int_{x_{0}^{\prime}}^{x_{T}^{\prime}}K_{y^{\prime},h^{\prime}}(z)dz\right|

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\leq\left(x_{T}-x_{0}\right)\|K_{y,h}-K_{y^{\prime},h^{\prime}}\|_{[-A,A]}

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt+\left|\int_{\min\{x_{0},x_{0}^{\prime}\}}^{\max\{x_{0},x_{0}^{\prime}\}}K_{y^{\prime},h^{\prime}}(z)dz+\int_{\min\{x_{T},x_{T}^{\prime}\}}^{\max\{x_{T},x_{T}^{\prime}\}}K_{y^{\prime},h^{\prime}}(z)dz\right|

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\leq\left(x_{T}-x_{0}\right)\|K_{y,h}-K_{y^{\prime},h^{\prime}}\|_{[-A,A]}+2\|x-x^{\prime}\|_{[0,T]}\|K\|_{[-1,1]}.

The second summand vanishes for
\|x-x^{\prime}\|_{[0,T]}\to 0
, the first one for
(y^{\prime},h^{\prime})\to(y,h)
because

\displaystyle\|K_{y,h}-K_{y^{\prime},h^{\prime}}\|_{[-A,A]}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{z\in[-A,A]}\|K^{\prime}\|_{[-1,1]}\left|\frac{z-y}{h}-\frac{z-y^{\prime}}{h^{\prime}}\right|

\displaystyle=\|K^{\prime}\|_{[-1,1]}\cdot\sup_{z\in[-A,A]}\left|\frac{z(h-h^{\prime})-yh^{\prime}+y^{\prime}h}{hh^{\prime}}\right|

tends to zero in this limiting scenario. Continuity of (
I.2
) follows.
∎

I.2
Preliminaries

Here, we first give some important preliminary results on the fractional calculus that are frequently used in the proofs of the results of Section
6
in the following subsections. In the second part we introduce a change of measure formula for fractional Brownian motion and present how the density process can be expressed in terms of fractional integrals.

I.2.1
Fractional calculus and hypergeometric function

First, we give some elementary definitions and results about fractional integrals and derivatives. The standard reference on this subject is
[
47
]
.

Let
\Gamma
denote the Gamma function.
The
(left-handed) Riemann–Liouville fractional integral

I_{0+}^{\alpha}
of order
\alpha>0
for any function
f\in L^{1}([0,T])
is defined as

\displaystyle\left(I_{0+}^{\alpha}f\right)(x):=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\alpha)}\int_{0}^{x}\frac{f(y)}{(x-y)^{1-\alpha}}dy.

(I.3)

The fractional derivative can be introduced as its inverse operator. Assuming
0<\alpha<1
and
p\geq 1
, we denote by
I_{0+}^{\alpha}(L^{p}([0,T]))
the image of
L^{p}([0,T])
with respect to the operator
I_{0+}^{\alpha}
. Then for
p>1
and for each
f\in I_{0+}^{\alpha}(L^{p}([0,T]))
, the function
g
with
f=I_{0+}^{\alpha}g
is unique in
L^{p}([0,T])
and coincides with the
(left-handed) Riemann–Liouville fractional derivative

D_{0+}^{\alpha}f
of
f
of order
\alpha
, which is for any
f\in L^{1}([0,T])
given almost everywhere by

\left(D_{0+}^{\alpha}f\right)(x):=\frac{1}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\frac{d}{dx}\int_{0}^{x}\frac{f(y)}{(x-y)^{\alpha}}dy.

On
I_{0+}^{\alpha}(L^{1}([0,T]))
, the fractional derivative has the so-called Weyl representation (cf. Corollary subsequent to Theorem
13.1
in
[
47
]
)

\displaystyle\left(D_{0+}^{\alpha}f\right)(x)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\left(\frac{f(x)}{x^{\alpha}}+\alpha\int_{0}^{x}\frac{f(x)-f(y)}{(x-y)^{1+\alpha}}dy\right)\hskip 10.00002pta.e.

(I.4)

Last but not least, we give two examples that are frequently used in the following subsections and can be found as
(2.26)
and
(2.44)
in
[
47
]
. For the function
f(x)=x^{-\mu}
and
0<\mu<1
we have

\displaystyle\left(D_{0+}^{\alpha}f\right)(x)=\frac{\Gamma(1-\mu)}{\Gamma(1-\mu-\alpha)}x^{-\mu-\alpha}

(I.5)

and for
f(x)=x^{\beta-1}
with
\beta>0
it holds that

\displaystyle\left(I_{0+}^{\alpha}f\right)(x)=\frac{\Gamma(\beta)}{\Gamma(\alpha+\beta)}x^{\alpha+\beta-1}.

(I.6)

The Gaussian hypergeometric function

F:\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}\times\left(\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0,-1,-2,\dots\}\right)\times\left(\mathbb{C}\setminus\{x\in\mathbb{R}:x\geq 1\}\right)\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}

is the analytic continuation of the power series

\displaystyle(a,b,c;z)\ \mapsto\ \sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(a)_{n}(b)_{n}}{(c)_{n}n!}z^{n},

(I.7)

with the Pochhammer symbol
(z)_{0}=1
and
(z)_{n}:=z(z+1)\cdots(z+n-1)
,
z\in\mathbb{C}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
(cf.
[
16
]
, Section
2
and
[
39
]
,
IV.20.3
).

Remark I.2

(Some properties of the hypergeometric function)
.

We give some properties of the Gaussian hypergeometric function
F(a,b,c;z)
with
(a,b,c,z)\in\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}\times\left(\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0,-1,-2,\dots\}\right)\times\left(\mathbb{C}\setminus\{x\in\mathbb{R}:x\geq 1\}\right)
that can be found in the literature and are used below.

(i)

For
\Re(c)>\Re(b)>0
, we have Euler’s formula

F(a,b,c;z)=\frac{\Gamma(a)}{\Gamma(b)\Gamma(c-b)}\int_{0}^{1}t^{b-1}(1-t)^{c-b-1}(1-tz)^{-a}dt,

cf.
(10)
in
[
23
]
, Section
2.1.3
, where
\Re(z)
denotes the real part of
z\in\mathbb{C}
.

(ii)

F
is commutative in its first two arguments i.e.

F(a,b,c;z)=F(b,a,c;z).

This is given in
2.1.2
in
[
23
]
for
|z|<1
and follows for the analytic continuation by the identity theorem for analytic functions, cf. Theorem
III.3.2
in
[
10
]
, applied to
z\mapsto F(a,b,c;z)-F(b,a,c;z)
.

(iii)

We have
F(a,0,c;z)=1
, which is immediate by the power series representation (
I.7
) inside the convergence disk
\{z\in\mathbb{C}:|z|<1\}
and follows for the analytic continuation by the identity theorem for analytic functions, cf. Theorem
III.3.2
in
[
10
]
.

(iv)

We have
F(H-1/2,1/2-H,H+1/2,x)\geq 0
for real arguments
x\leq 0
. This follows for
H<\frac{1}{2}
by Euler’s formula in (i) and for
H>\frac{1}{2}
correspondingly when first applying the commutativity relationship (ii). The case
H=\frac{1}{2}
is obtained from (iii).

I.2.2
Fractional Brownian motion

Let
\Gamma
denote the Gamma function and define the kernel
K_{H}
for
s\leq t
and
H\in(0,1)
by

\displaystyle K_{H}(t,s):=\frac{(t-s)^{H-\frac{1}{2}}}{\Gamma\left(H+\frac{1}{2}\right)}F\left(H-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}-H,H+\frac{1}{2};1-\frac{t}{s}\right),

(I.8)

where
F
denotes the Gaussian hypergeometric function, see Subsection
I.2.1
.
The covariance function
R_{H}
of a fractional Brownian motion in (
6.3
) has a representation of the form (see
[
40
]
, p.
106
and
[
16
]
, Lemma
3.1
)

\displaystyle R_{H}(t,s)=\int_{0}^{t\wedge s}K_{H}(t,u)K_{H}(s,u)du

(I.9)

and it follows from this that the process
W^{H}=(W_{t}^{H})_{t\in[0,T]}
with

W_{t}^{H}:=\int_{0}^{t}K_{H}(t,s)dW_{s},\hskip 10.00002ptt\in[0,T],

is a fractional Brownian motion on the interval
[0,T]
. By
R_{1/2}(s,t)=s\wedge t
, we see that
W^{H}|_{H=1/2}=W
is a standard Brownian motion.

To move on to a change of measure formula for the fractional Brownian motion, we introduce the operator
K_{H}
associated with the kernel
K_{H}(\cdot,\cdot)
on
L^{2}([0,T])
that is given by

\left(K_{H}f\right)(t):=\int_{0}^{t}K_{H}(t,s)f(s)ds.

This operator is an isomorphism from
L^{2}([0,T])
onto
I_{0+}^{H+\frac{1}{2}}(L^{2}([0,T])
(cf.
[
47
]
, Theorem
10.4
).
It can be expressed for
f\in L^{2}([0,T])
in terms of fractional integrals in the following way:

\displaystyle\left(K_{H}f\right)(t)=\begin{cases}I_{0+}^{2H}\left((\cdot)^{\frac{1}{2}-H}I_{0+}^{\frac{1}{2}-H}\left((\cdot)^{H-\frac{1}{2}}f(\cdot)\right)(\cdot)\right)(t)&\quad\textrm{ for }H<\frac{1}{2},\\
I_{0+}^{1}\left((\cdot)^{H-\frac{1}{2}}I_{0+}^{H-\frac{1}{2}}\left((\cdot)^{\frac{1}{2}-H}f(\cdot)\right)(\cdot)\right)(t)&\quad\textrm{ for }H>\frac{1}{2},\end{cases}

(I.10)

see equation
(7)
and
(8)
in
[
40
]
and Theorem
10.4
in
[
47
]
. For absolutely continuous
f\in I_{0+}^{H+1/2}\left(L^{2}([0,T])\right)
, its inverse operator
K_{H}^{-1}
can be expressed as

\displaystyle\left(K_{H}^{-1}f\right)(s)=\begin{cases}s^{H-\frac{1}{2}}I_{0+}^{\frac{1}{2}-H}\left((\cdot)^{\frac{1}{2}-H}f^{\prime}(\cdot)\right)(s)&\quad\textrm{ for }H<\frac{1}{2},\\
s^{H-\frac{1}{2}}D_{0+}^{H-\frac{1}{2}}\left((\cdot)^{\frac{1}{2}-H}f^{\prime}(\cdot)\right)(s)&\quad\textrm{ for }H>\frac{1}{2},\end{cases}

(I.11)

with weak derivative
f^{\prime}
, see equation
(11)
and
(13)
in
[
40
]
where a derivation is given.

With the help of this inverse operator
K_{H}^{-1}
we can now give a formulation of Girsanov’s theorem for the fractional Brownian motion. Other sources on change of measure for fractional Brownian motion are
[
16
]
and
[
40
]
.

Proposition I.3
(Theorem
1
in
[
52
]
).

Let
u=(u_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
be an adapted process with integrable trajectories and set

\tilde{W}_{t}^{H}:=W_{t}^{H}+\int_{0}^{t}u_{s}ds.

Assume that

(a)

\int_{0}^{\cdot}u_{s}ds\in I_{0+}^{H+\frac{1}{2}}(L^{2}([0,T]))
almost surely,

(b)

\mathbb{E}[Z_{T}^{H}(u)]=1
, where

\displaystyle Z_{T}^{H}(u)=\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{T}K_{H}^{-1}\left(\int_{0}^{\cdot}u_{r}dr\right)(s)dW_{s}\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\left.-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}K_{H}^{-1}\left(\int_{0}^{\cdot}u_{r}dr\right)^{2}(s)ds\right).

Then the shifted process
\tilde{W}^{H}
is a fractional Brownian motion with Hurst parameter
H
under the new probability measure
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
defined by
\frac{d\tilde{\mathbb{P}}}{d\mathbb{P}}=Z_{T}^{H}(u)
and the process

W_{t}+\int_{0}^{t}K_{H}^{-1}\left(\int_{0}^{\cdot}u_{r}dr\right)(s)ds

is a Brownian motion.

Remark I.4
.

Note that from the representation (
I.11
) and the form of the fractional operators in (
I.3
) and (
I.4
) it can be seen that the density process
Z_{T}^{H}(u)
in Proposition
I.3
is adapted for
u_{s}:=g(X_{s}^{H,b})
, where
X^{H,b}
solves (
6.2
) and
g
is a measurable function. If
g
is in particular Lipschitz continuous, then Proposition
1
in
[
52
]
reveals that

\left(K_{H}^{-1}\left(\int_{0}^{\cdot}g(X_{s}^{H,b})ds\right)(t)\right)_{t\in[0,T]}\in L^{2}([0,T]).

Thus, the stochastic integral in Proposition
I.3
is well-defined in the classical Itô sense.

Remark I.5
.

In our paper we work under the condition
u_{s}=g(X^{H,b}_{s})
for a bounded and Lipschitz continuous function
g
and need condition (a) in Proposition
I.3
to be satisfied for those.

For
H<\frac{1}{2}
, this condition (a) follows from
\int_{0}^{T}u_{s}^{2}ds<\infty
, which was derived in
[
5
]
within the proof of Lemma
4.1
on p. 313–314. Since
g
is bounded, this follows trivially for
u=g(X_{\cdot}^{H,b})
. For
H>\frac{1}{2}
, at the beginning of Section
5
on p. 316–317 it has been derived that condition (a) is satisfied if
u
has trajectories that are Hölder continuous of order
H-\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon
for some
\epsilon>0
. This is true for
u=g(X_{\cdot}^{H,b})
due to the Hölder continuity of order
H-\epsilon
with arbitrary
\epsilon\in(0,H)
of the fractional Brownian motion
W^{H}
and the Lipschitz assumption on
g
.

From the SDE (
6.2
) we directly get

X_{t}^{H,b}-X_{t}^{b}=\int_{0}^{t}\big(b(X_{s}^{H,b})-b(X_{s}^{b})\big)ds+\sigma(W_{t}^{H}-W_{t}).

As each
b\in\Sigma(C,A,\gamma,\sigma)\cap\mathcal{H}(1,L)
is Lipschitz with constant
L
, we derive

|X_{t}^{H,b}-X_{t}^{b}|\leq L\int_{0}^{t}|X_{s}^{H,b}-X_{s}^{b}|ds+\sigma|W_{t}^{H}-W_{t}|.

From Gronwall’s lemma (cf.
(2.11)
in
[
28
]
, Chapter
5
with corresponding proof on p. 387–388) we then get

|X_{t}^{H,b}-X_{t}^{b}|\leq\sigma|W_{t}^{H}-W_{t}|+L\int_{0}^{t}|W_{s}^{H}-W_{s}|e^{L(t-s)}ds

and in consequence

\|X^{H,b}-X^{b}\|_{[0,T]}\leq\|W^{H}-W\|_{[0,T]}\left(\sigma+L\int_{0}^{t}e^{L(t-s)}ds\right).

Note that the right-hand side does not depend on
b
anymore. Because
\|W^{H}-W\|_{[0,T]}
converges to zero in probability, which was shown in Section
3.8.3
of
[
9
]
, the claim follows.
∎

I.3
Proof of Theorem
6.3

First, we provide some helpful preliminary results. At the end of this subsection, the proofs of Theorem
6.3
and Remark
6.4
are given.

Lemma I.6
(Uniform continuous mapping).

Let
(E,d)
be a separable metric space and
f:E\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
a uniformly continuous function. Suppose for a parameter space
\Theta
and
E
-valued random variables
X,X_{1},X_{2},\dots
we have uniform stochastic convergence, i.e. for every
\epsilon>0
,

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(d(X_{n},X)>\epsilon\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}0.

Then we also have for every
\epsilon>0
that

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(|f(X_{n})-f(X)|>\epsilon\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}0.

By the uniform continuity of
f
, for every
\epsilon>0
we can find
\delta_{\epsilon}>0
such that
|f(x)-f(y)|\leq\epsilon
whenever
d(x,y)\leq\delta_{\epsilon}
. Then

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(|f(X_{n})-f(X)|>\epsilon\right)\leq\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left(d(X_{n},X)>\delta_{\epsilon}\right)

and the right-hand side converges to zero by assumption.
∎

From here on, we define for
c\in\mathbb{R}
the set

\displaystyle D_{c,T}:=\{f\in\mathcal{C}([0,T])\mid-c,c\in\textrm{im}(f)\}.

(I.12)

Lemma I.7
.

There exists
\epsilon>0
such that

\lim_{T\to\infty}\inf_{b\in\Sigma(C,A,\gamma,\sigma)\cap\mathcal{H}(1,L)}\mathbb{P}\left(X^{b}\in D_{A+\epsilon,T}\right)=1.

By
X_{0}^{b}=x_{0}\in[-A,A]
, continuity of paths and the occupation times formula, we have for any
\epsilon>0

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(X^{b}\in D_{A+\epsilon,T}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\geq\mathbb{P}\left(\int_{0}^{T}\mathbbm{1}_{(A+\epsilon,A+2\epsilon]}(X_{s}^{b})ds>0\textrm{ and }\int_{0}^{T}\mathbbm{1}_{[-A-2\epsilon,-A-\epsilon)}(X_{s}^{b})ds>0\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=\mathbb{P}\left(T\int_{A+\epsilon}^{A+2\epsilon}q_{b}(z)dz+T\int_{A+\epsilon}^{A+2\epsilon}\frac{1}{T\sigma^{2}}L_{T}^{z}(X^{b})-q_{b}(z)dz>0\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\left.\textrm{ and }T\int_{-A-2\epsilon}^{-A-\epsilon}q_{b}(z)dz+T\int_{-A-2\epsilon}^{-A-\epsilon}\frac{1}{T\sigma^{2}}L_{T}^{z}(X^{b})-q_{b}(z)dz>0\right).

Now we specify
\epsilon:=L_{*}/(4L^{*})
. Lemma
D.1
reveals that for all
A\leq y\leq A+2\epsilon
and all
-A-2\epsilon\leq z\leq-A
,

\left|q_{b}(A)-q_{b}(y)\right|\leq 2L^{*}\epsilon\leq\frac{L_{*}}{2}\ \textrm{ and }\ \left|q_{b}(-A)-q_{b}(z)\right|\leq 2L^{*}\epsilon\leq\frac{L_{*}}{2}.

Consequently, as
q_{b}(A),q_{b}(-A)\geq L_{*}
by Lemma
D.1
,

\int_{A+\epsilon}^{A+2\epsilon}q_{b}(z)dz\geq\frac{(L_{*})^{2}}{8L^{*}}\ \ \textrm{ and }\ \ \int_{-A-2\epsilon}^{-A-\epsilon}q_{b}(z)dz\geq\frac{(L_{*})^{2}}{8L^{*}}

and we can conclude

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(X^{b}\in D_{A+\epsilon,T}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}\left(\epsilon\left\|\frac{1}{T\sigma^{2}}L_{T}^{\cdot}(X)-q_{b}\right\|_{\infty}<\frac{(L_{*})^{2}}{16L^{*}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(\left\|\frac{1}{T\sigma^{2}}L_{T}^{\cdot}(X)-q_{b}\right\|_{\infty}<\frac{L_{*}}{4}\right).

In particular,

\displaystyle\liminf_{T\to\infty}\inf_{b\in\Sigma(C,A,\gamma,\sigma)\cap\mathcal{H}(1,L)}\mathbb{P}\left(X^{b}\in D_{A+\epsilon,T}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\geq\liminf_{T\to\infty}\inf_{b\in\Sigma(C,A,\gamma,\sigma)\cap\mathcal{H}(1,L)}\mathbb{P}\left(\left\|\frac{1}{T\sigma^{2}}L_{T}^{\cdot}(X)-q_{b}\right\|_{\infty}<\frac{L_{*}}{4}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=1-\limsup_{T\to\infty}\sup_{b\in\Sigma(C,A,\gamma,\sigma)\cap\mathcal{H}(1,L)}\mathbb{P}\left(\left\|\frac{1}{T\sigma^{2}}L_{T}^{\cdot}(X)-q_{b}\right\|_{\infty}\geq\frac{L_{*}}{4}\right)

and the right-hand side equals one by (
D.1
).
∎

Lemma I.8
.

The set
D_{A,T}\subset\mathcal{C}([0,T])
is closed with respect to the topology of uniform convergence.

Consider a sequence
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
with
f_{n}\in D_{A,T}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, that converges to some function
f\in\mathcal{C}([0,T])
, i.e.
\|f_{n}-f\|_{[0,T]}\rightarrow 0
. Because of
f_{n}\in D_{A,T}
, there exists a sequence
(t_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
with
f_{n}(t_{n})=A
. By compactness of
[0,T]
, we find a subsequence
(t_{n_{k}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
with
t_{n_{k}}\rightarrow t
for
k\to\infty
and some
t\in[0,T]
. For this
t
, we have

\displaystyle|f(t)-A|=\left|f(t)-f_{n_{k}}(t_{n_{k}})\right|\leq\left|f(t)-f(t_{n_{k}})\right|+\left|f(t_{n_{k}})-f_{n_{k}}(t_{n_{k}})\right|.

The first summand vanishes in the limit
k\to\infty
by continuity of
f
and the second one by
\|f_{n_{k}}-f\|_{[0,T]}\rightarrow 0
. Thus,
A\in\textrm{im}(f)
. By the same reasoning, we see that
-A\in\textrm{im}(f)
and conclude
f\in D_{A,T}
.
∎

Lemma I.9
.

Let
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of real numbers,
a\in\mathbb{R}
and
|a_{n}-a|\to 0
. Let
(b_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence with
b_{n}\in\{a_{k}:k\in\mathbb{N}\}
,
b\in\mathbb{R}
, and
|b_{n}-b|\to 0
. Then one of the following is true:

(i)

a=b
, or

(ii)

there exists
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
b_{n}=b
for all
n\geq n_{0}
.

Suppose (i) does not hold and choose
0<\epsilon<|b-a|
. As
b
is the limit of
(b_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, there exists
n_{0}
such that
|b-b_{n}|<\frac{\epsilon}{2}
for all
n\geq n_{0}
, in particular
|b_{n}-a|>\frac{\epsilon}{2}
for
n\geq n_{0}
. As there are only finitely many
a_{n_{1}},\dots,a_{n_{l}}
with
|a_{n_{i}}-a|>\frac{\epsilon}{2}
,
i=1,\dots,l
, we have
b_{n}\in\{a_{n_{1}},\dots,a_{n_{l}}\}=:A
for all
n\geq n_{0}
. We conclude that
(b_{n})_{n\geq n_{0}}
is a convergent
A
-valued sequence and as
A
only contains isolated points, (ii) holds.
∎

The following result is well-known, yet we did not find a version simplified for our context. For completeness, the proof is given.

Lemma I.10
.

The set
\Sigma(C,A,\gamma,\sigma)\cap\mathcal{H}(1,L)
is relatively compact with respect to the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets.

Let
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence in
\Sigma(C,A,\gamma,\sigma)\cap\mathcal{H}(1,L)
. We have to prove that there exists a subsequence which is convergent in
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R})
with respect to the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets.
For any compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}
, the restriction of
\Sigma(C,A,\gamma,\sigma)\cap\mathcal{H}(1,L)
to
K
is relatively compact with respect to the topology of uniform convergence on
K
by the Arzelà–Ascoli theorem. For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
K_{n}:=[-n,n]
. Note that

\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}K_{n}=\mathbb{R}.

For
K_{1}
we find a subsequence
\big(n_{k}^{(1)}\big)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
and an
f_{1}\in\mathcal{C}(K_{1})
such that

\left\|f_{n_{k}^{(1)}}-f_{1}\right\|_{K_{1}}\rightarrow 0\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{ for }k\to\infty.

We proceed inductively as follows: If
\big(n_{k}^{(i)}\big)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
and
f_{i}
are chosen, we find a subsequence
\big(n_{k}^{(i+1)}\big)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
of
\big(n_{k}^{(i)}\big)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
and
f_{i+1}
such that

\left\|f_{n_{k}^{(i+1)}}-f_{i+1}\right\|_{K_{i+1}}\rightarrow 0\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{ for }k\to\infty.

Note that
f_{i}
restricted to
K_{j}
with
j<i
coincides with
f_{j}
on
K_{j}
because
K_{j}\subset K_{i}
. Hence, there exists
f\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R})
with
f
restricted to
K_{i}
equals
f_{i}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Next, as every compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}
is (closed and) bounded, there exists
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
K\subset K_{n}
. Finally, an application of Cantor’s diagonal subsequence argument reveals the diagonal sequence
(m_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
with
m_{k}:=n_{k}^{(k)}
such that for every compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}
,

\left\|f_{m_{k}}-f\right\|_{K}\rightarrow 0\hskip 10.00002pt\textrm{ for }k\to\infty.

∎

Here and subsequently, the image measure of a
\mathcal{C}([0,T])
-valued random variable
Y
is denoted by
\mathbb{P}^{Y}
.

Lemma I.11
.

Let
L>0
and
(h_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence with
h_{n}\in(0,1)
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
|h_{n}-\frac{1}{2}|\to 0
. Then the family of measures

\left\{\mathbb{P}^{X^{H,b}}:b\in\Sigma(C,A,\gamma,\sigma)\cap\mathcal{H}(1,L),H\in\{h_{n}:n\in\mathbb{N}\}\right\}

is tight.

Consider a sequence
(b_{n},H_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
with
b_{n}\in\Sigma(C,A,\gamma,\sigma)\cap\mathcal{H}(1,L)
and
H_{n}\in\{h_{k}:k\in\mathbb{N}\}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. As
\Sigma(C,A,\gamma,\sigma)\cap\mathcal{H}(1,L)
is relatively compact with respect to the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets as a consequence of the Arzelà–Ascoli theorem (see Lemma
I.10
) and
(H_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
contains a convergent subsequence by the Bolzano–Weierstrass theorem as it is a bounded sequence, we find a subsequence
(b_{n_{k}},H_{n_{k}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
with
\|b_{n_{k}}-b\|_{K}\to 0
for any compact
K\subset\mathbb{R}
and
|H_{n_{k}}-H|\to 0
. We have

\displaystyle\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{BL}(\mathcal{C}([0,T]))}\hskip-1.42271pt\mathbb{E}\left[\left|f(X^{H_{n_{k}},b_{n_{k}}})-f(X^{H,b})\right|\right]\hskip-1.42271pt\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\|X^{H_{n_{k}},b_{n_{k}}}-X^{H,b}\|_{[0,T]}\wedge 2\right]

(I.13)

by the definition (
F.1
) of
\mathcal{F}_{BL}
in Subsection
F.1
. In order to conclude that the right-hand side converges to zero by the dominated convergence theorem, we have to show that
\|X^{H_{n_{k}},b_{n_{k}}}-X^{H,b}\|_{[0,T]}\wedge 2
converges to zero pointwise, i.e.
\omega
-wise. For each
\omega\in\Omega
, there exists a compact set
K=K(\omega)\subset\mathbb{R}
such that
X_{t}^{H,b}(\omega)\in K
for all
t\in[0,T]
by continuity of
X^{H,b}(\omega)
. Then for
t\leq T
,

\displaystyle\left|X_{t}^{H_{n_{k}},b_{n_{k}}}-X_{t}^{H,b}\right|

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq\int_{0}^{t}\left|b_{n_{k}}(X_{s}^{H_{n_{k}},b_{n_{k}}})-b(X_{s}^{H,b})\right|ds+\sigma\left|W_{t}^{H_{n_{k}}}-W_{t}^{H}\right|
